- **authority**: State ex rel. Heartland Title Services, Inc. v. Harrell, 500 S.W.3d 239 (Mo. banc 2016) [full-text] (footnote 6 not re-read in this task; proposition from earlier pipeline)

### Computation of time (civil rules)


Exclude the day of the event; include the last day unless it is a weekend or legal holiday, then the next business day; under seven days, intermediate weekends and holidays are excluded.

- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 44.01(a), verbatim: "(a) Computation. In computing any period of time prescribed or allowed by these rules, by order of court, or by any applicable statute, the day of the act, event, or default after which the designated period of time begins to run is not to be included. The last day of the period so computed is to be included, unless it is a Saturday, Sunday or a legal holiday, in which event the period runs until the end of the next day which is neither a Saturday, Sunday nor a legal holiday. When the period of time prescribed or allowed is less than seven days, intermediate Saturdays, Sundays and legal holidays shall be excluded in the computation."

### Enlargement of time


A court may extend a deadline for cause if asked before it expires, or after expiry on motion and notice for excusable neglect, except for deadlines the rule lists as non-extendable.

- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 44.01(b), verbatim: "(b) Enlargement. When by these rules or by a notice given thereunder or by order of court an act is required or allowed to be done at or within a specified time, the court for cause shown may at any time in its discretion (1) with or without motion or notice order the period enlarged if request therefor is made before the expiration of the period originally prescribed or as extended by a previous order or (2) upon notice and motion made after the expiration of the specified period permit the act to be done where the failure to act was the result of excusable neglect; but it may not extend the time for taking any action under rules 52.13, 72.01, 73.01, 75.01, 78.04, 81.04, 81.07, and 84.035 or for commencing civil action."

### Answer (time to plead)

- **definition**: The defendant's first pleading responding to the petition; due 30 days after service of summons and petition (personal service).
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 55.25(a), verbatim: "(a) Answer - When Filed. A defendant shall file an answer within thirty days after the service of the summons and petition, except where service by mail is had, in which event a defendant shall file an answer within thirty days after the acknowledgment of receipt of summons and petition or return registered or certified mail receipt is filed in the case or within forty-five days after the first publication of notice if neither personal service nor service by mail is had."

### Interlocutory order of default

- **definition**: A preliminary court order declaring a party in default for failing to plead or otherwise defend; a default judgment may then be entered.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 74.05(b), verbatim: "(b) Entry of Interlocutory Order of Default. When a party against whom a judgment for affirmative relief is sought has failed to plead or otherwise defend as provided by these rules, an interlocutory order of default may be entered against that party. After entry of an interlocutory order of default, a default judgment may be entered. Any party may demand a jury to assess damages. If a jury is not demanded, the court shall assess any damages."

### Good cause (set aside default)

- **definition**: A mistake, or conduct that is not intentionally or recklessly designed to impede the judicial process.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 74.05(d), verbatim: ""Good cause" includes a mistake or conduct that is not intentionally or recklessly designed to impede the judicial process."

### Strong preference for deciding on the merits

- **definition**: Missouri courts disfavor default judgments and lean toward setting them aside so cases are decided on their merits.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Brungard v. Risky's Inc., 240 S.W.3d 685 (Mo. banc 2007) [full-text]

### Request for admission

- **definition**: A written request that the other side admit facts or the genuineness of documents; unanswered requests in non-Chapter-517 cases are deemed admitted.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 59.01(a)(2), verbatim: "(2) Except in cases where the provisions of Chapter 517 apply, a failure to timely respond to requests for admissions in compliance with this Rule 59.01 shall result in each matter being admitted."

### Premature request for admission

- **definition**: A request served before the defendant's 30-day waiting period is invalid, as if never served.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Lane House Construction, Inc. v. Ogrowsky, 427 S.W.3d 882 (Mo. App. 2014) (district not confirmed) [full-text]

### Amendment as of course

- **definition**: The one amendment of a pleading a party may make without permission before a responsive pleading is served.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 55.33(a), verbatim: "(a) Amendments. A pleading may be amended once as a matter of course at any time before a responsive pleading is served or, if the pleading is one to which no responsive pleading is permitted and the action has not been placed upon the trial calendar, the pleading may be amended at any time within thirty days after it is served."

### Relation back

- **definition**: An amended claim from the same conduct or occurrence is treated as filed on the original date for limitation purposes.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 55.33(c), verbatim: "(c) Relation Back of Amendments. Whenever the claim or defense asserted in the amended pleading arose out of the conduct, transaction, or occurrence set forth or attempted to be set forth in the original pleading, the amendment relates back to the date of the original pleading."

### Summary judgment


A ruling without trial when the movant shows no genuine dispute of material fact and a right to judgment; a 'genuine issue' needs competent materials showing two plausible, contradictory accounts.

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English; ITT Commercial Finance v. Mid-America Marine (full-text for this passage; current Rule 74.04 not read)

### Genuine issue of material fact

- **definition**: A real, non-frivolous dispute supported by competent evidence about a fact that matters to the claim or defense.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: ITT Commercial Finance Corp. v. Mid-America Marine Supply Corp., 854 S.W.2d 371 (Mo. banc 1993) [full-text]

### Service of process / return of service


Formal delivery of the summons and petition; the officer's signed return states time, place and manner and fixes the date from which the answer period runs (Rule 54.20, not quoted).

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English; Rule 54.13/54.20 text not re-read in this task

### In forma pauperis (IFP)

- **definition**: Permission to proceed without paying court fees because of inability to pay; federal standard in 28 U.S.C. 1915; Missouri state IFP statute not verified here.
- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English; 28 U.S.C. 1915 text read

### Conversion


Taking or using someone's personal property as your own in opposition to the owner's rights; proved by a tortious taking, an appropriation showing a claim of right against the owner, or refusal to return on demand.

- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Emerick v. Mutual Benefit Life Insurance Co., 756 S.W.2d 513 (Mo. banc 1988) [full-text]

### Bailment / bailee


An arrangement where the owner (bailor) delivers property to another (bailee) for a purpose, such as towing and storage, and the bailee must return it or deliver it as the owner directs; Missouri authority on delivery to a non-owner was not located.

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English; no verified Missouri bailment case

### Negligence per se


Using a statute's violation as proof of negligence, but only if the injured party is in the protected class, the harm is the kind the statute targets, and the violation proximately caused the injury.

- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Lowdermilk v. Vescovo Building & Realty Co., 91 S.W.3d 617 (Mo. App. E.D. 2002) [full-text]

### Civil conspiracy

- **definition**: An agreement to do an unlawful act or use unlawful means to do a lawful act; it fails if the underlying tort fails.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Oak Bluff Partners, Inc. v. Meyer, 3 S.W.3d 777 (Mo. banc 1999) (per curiam) [full-text]

### Unjust enrichment

- **definition**: A claim that someone received and kept a benefit under circumstances making retention unjust; not available where the parties got what they bargained for.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Howard v. Turnbull, 316 S.W.3d 431 (Mo. App. W.D. 2010) [full-text]

### Merchandise (MMPA)

- **definition**: Objects, wares, goods, commodities, intangibles, real estate or services.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 407.010(4), verbatim: "(4) "Merchandise", any objects, wares, goods, commodities, intangibles, real estate or services;"

### Trade or commerce (MMPA)

- **definition**: Advertising, offering for sale, sale or distribution of services and property of any kind, including trade affecting people of Missouri.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 407.010(7), verbatim: "(7) "Trade" or "commerce", the advertising, offering for sale, sale, or distribution, or any combination thereof, of any services and any property, tangible or intangible, real, personal, or mixed, and any other article, commodity, or thing of value wherever situated. The terms "trade" and "commerce" include any trade or commerce directly or indirectly affecting the people of this state."

### Unlawful practice (MMPA)


Deception, fraud, false pretense, false promise, misrepresentation, unfair practice or concealment of a material fact in connection with the sale or advertisement of merchandise.

- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 407.020.1, verbatim: "1. The act, use or employment by any person of any deception, fraud, false pretense, false promise, misrepresentation, unfair practice or the concealment, suppression, or omission of any material fact in connection with the sale or advertisement of any merchandise in trade or commerce or the solicitation of any funds for any charitable purpose, as defined in section 407.453, in or from the state of Missouri, is declared to be an unlawful practice. The use by any person, in connection with the sale or advertisement of any merchandise in trade or commerce or the solicitation of any funds for any charitable purpose, as defined in section 407.453, in or from the state of Missouri of the fact that the attorney general has approved any filing required by this chapter as the approval, sanction or endorsement of any activity, project or action of such person, is declared to be an unlawful practice."

### Ascertainable loss (MMPA private action)

- **definition**: The monetary or property loss a purchaser must prove, along with reasonable-consumer conduct and definite, objective damages.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 407.025.1, verbatim: "1. (1) Any person who purchases or leases merchandise primarily for personal, family or household purposes and thereby suffers an ascertainable loss of money or property, real or personal, as a result of the use or employment by another person of a method, act or practice declared unlawful by section 407.020, may bring a private civil action in either the circuit court of the county in which the seller or lessor resides or in which the transaction complained of took place, to recover actual damages."

### Abandoned property (towing statutes)


An unattended motor vehicle or similar property removed or subject to removal from public or private property under sections 304.155 and 304.157, whether or not operational.

- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 304.001(1), verbatim: "(1) "Abandoned property", any unattended motor vehicle, trailer, all-terrain vehicle, outboard motor or vessel removed or subject to removal from public or private property as provided in sections 304.155 and 304.157, whether or not operational. For any vehicle towed from the scene of an accident at the request of law enforcement and not retrieved by the vehicle's owner within five days of the accident, the agency requesting the tow shall be required to write an abandoned property report or a crime inquiry and inspection report;"

### Towing company

- **definition**: Any person or entity that tows, removes or stores abandoned property.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 304.001(14), verbatim: "(14) "Towing company", any person or entity which tows, removes or stores abandoned property;"

### Possessory lien (tow operator)


A right to keep the vehicle until reasonable towing and storage charges are paid; under 304.155.9 it lasts until possession is voluntarily relinquished to the owner or a secured party. Effect of release to a non-owner is unresearched.

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English summary of RSMo 304.155.9 (text read)

### Defamation (actual damages required)


A false statement of fact, published to a third party, that harms reputation; Missouri abolished the per se/per quod distinction and requires proof of actual damages in all cases.

- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Nazeri v. Missouri Valley College, 860 S.W.2d 303 (Mo. banc 1993) [full-text]

### Qualified privilege (reports to police)


Good-faith communications to law enforcement to help bring a criminal to justice are privileged unless the plaintiff proves malice (bad faith); whether the privilege exists is for the court.

- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Rucker v. K-Mart Corp., 734 S.W.2d 533 (Mo. App. E.D. 1987) [full-text] ; privilege-is-for-the-court point: Overcast v. Billings Mutual (full-text)

### Malice (defamation/malicious prosecution)


Knowing falsity or reckless disregard in the defamation-privilege setting; in malicious prosecution, intentionally doing a wrongful act without legal justification, which may be inferred from the facts showing lack of probable cause.

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English; Sanders v. Daniel International (full-text) for malicious prosecution

### Malicious prosecution (elements)

- **definition**: Six elements: commencement of a prosecution; instigation by the defendant; termination in plaintiff's favor; want of probable cause; malice; damages.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Sanders v. Daniel International Corp., 682 S.W.2d 803 (Mo. banc 1984) [full-text]

### False imprisonment


Confinement without legal justification; those who directly procure, aid or assist an unlawful imprisonment can be liable, but a reporter whose complaint is followed by an officer's independent decision to arrest is generally not.

- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Rustici v. Weidemeyer, 673 S.W.2d 762 (Mo. banc 1984) [full-text] ; reporter-liability limit: Highfill v. Hale (full-text)

### Probable cause


A reasonable, fact-based belief that a crime was committed by the suspect; lack of it is an element of malicious prosecution and a Fourth Amendment issue in 1983 arrest claims.

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English explanation

### Statute of limitations / accrual


The deadline to sue, and the event that starts it; for conversion five years (RSMo 516.120(4)); for defamation, false imprisonment and malicious prosecution two years (RSMo 516.140).

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English summary of RSMo 516.120 and 516.140 (text read)

### Collateral estoppel / preclusion


A final decision on an issue (such as a conviction) can bar relitigating that issue in a later case; case-file Note cites James v. Paul for the risk in Tyler's civil suit against Brown (holding not confirmed).

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English; James v. Paul existence-only

### Record on appeal

- **definition**: Everything the appellate court may look at: the legal file (pleadings, orders, judgment, notice of appeal) and the transcript of the hearing.
- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English summary of Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.12 (text read)

### Time to file the record on appeal

- **definition**: The record must reach the appellate court within 30 days of the notice of appeal if it is only a legal file, and 90 days otherwise.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.19, verbatim: "The record on appeal shall be filed with the appellate court:
(a) If the record on appeal consists only of a legal file, within thirty days from the date of the filing of the notice of appeal in the trial court;
(b) In all other cases, within ninety days from the date of the filing of the notice of appeal in the trial court."

### Extension of time for the record

- **definition**: The appellate court may enlarge or shorten the time for filing the record on appeal, on application or on its own motion.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.20, verbatim: "The appellate court, either on application or on its own motion, may enlarge or shorten the time for filing the record on appeal."

### Transcript order duty


The appellant must order the transcript in writing within ten days after the notice of appeal and file a certificate of order and payment dates within ten days after paying.

- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.12(c)(1), verbatim: "(1) Duty of Appellant to Order Transcript. Within ten days after the notice of appeal is filed, appellant shall order the transcript, in writing, from the reporter or from the clerk of the trial court if the proceedings were recorded by means of an electronic sound recording. Charges due for preparation of the transcript shall be paid as directed in section 512.050, RSMo. The written order shall designate the portions of the proceedings and evidence not previously reduced to written form that are to be included in the transcript. Appellant's certificate stating the date on which the transcript was ordered and the date on which the transcript charges were paid shall be filed in the appellate court within ten days after the payment of the charges."

### Point relied on

- **definition**: The short, formula-based statement of each claimed trial error that begins an argument section in an appellant's brief.
- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English summary of Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 84.04(d) (text read)

### Plain error


An error affecting substantial rights that the court may reach in its discretion even if not preserved, if manifest injustice or a miscarriage of justice resulted.

- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 84.13(c), verbatim: "(c) Plain Error may be Considered. Plain errors affecting substantial rights may be considered on appeal, in the discretion of the court, though not raised or preserved, when the court finds that manifest injustice or miscarriage of justice has resulted therefrom."

### Standard of review (court-tried case)

- **definition**: The appellate court reviews law and evidence as in equity cases, giving due regard to the trial judge's chance to judge witness credibility.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 84.13(d)(1)-(2), verbatim: "(d) Appellate review in cases tried without a jury or with an advisory jury.
(1) The court shall review the case upon both the law and the evidence as in suits of an equitable nature;
(2) The court shall give due regard to the opportunity of the trial court to have judged the credibility of witnesses;"

### Weight-of-the-evidence review


A court-tried judgment stands unless unsupported by substantial evidence, against the weight of the evidence, or wrongly declares or applies the law; reversal on weight grounds is used cautiously.

- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Murphy v. Carron, 536 S.W.2d 30 (Mo. banc 1976) [full-text]

### Preservation of error


Raising an objection or argument in the trial court so the appellate court can consider it; Rule 84.04(e) requires each argument to say whether and how it was preserved.

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English summary of Rules 84.04(e) and 84.13(a) (text read)

### Continuance (written motion with affidavit)

- **definition**: A request to postpone a hearing must be in writing with an affidavit unless the other side consents; without that, denial is not an abuse of discretion.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: S.J.H. v. J.P.H., No. ED112072 (Mo. App. E.D. Oct. 1, 2024) [full-text]

### Abuse of discretion

- **definition**: A ruling so arbitrary or unreasonable it shows a lack of careful consideration; the usual review standard for continuances and for setting aside defaults.
- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English explanation; standard named in Brungard and S.J.H.

### Mootness


An appeal becomes moot when a decision could no longer affect the parties, for example after an order expires; RSMo 455.007 supplies a public-interest exception for full orders of protection.

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English; RSMo 455.007 (text read)

### Color of law

- **definition**: Acting with authority borrowed from the state, such as an on-duty officer; required for a 1983 claim.
- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English; 42 U.S.C. 1983 (text read)

### Heck bar

- **definition**: A rule that bars a 1983 damages claim that would necessarily show a still-standing conviction or sentence is invalid.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Heck v. Humphrey, 512 U.S. 477 (1994) [existence-only]

### Wallace accrual

- **definition**: A false-arrest claim starts its limitations clock when the person is held under legal process, even though related charges are still pending.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Wallace v. Kato, 549 U.S. 384 (2007) [existence-only]

### Monell liability

- **definition**: A city or county can be sued under 1983 only for its own official policy or custom, not merely because it employs a wrongdoer.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Monell v. New York City Department of Social Services, 436 U.S. 658 (1978) [existence-only]

### Final policymaker (Pembaur)

- **definition**: A single decision by an official with final authority over the subject can be the government's policy.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Pembaur v. City of Cincinnati, 475 U.S. 469 (1986) [existence-only]

### Absolute prosecutorial immunity

- **definition**: Prosecutors cannot be sued for damages for decisions to charge and for presenting the State's case.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Imbler v. Pachtman, 424 U.S. 409 (1976) [existence-only]

### Qualified immunity

- **definition**: Officials are shielded from damages unless they violated clearly established rights that a reasonable official would have known.
- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800 (1982) [existence-only]

### Franks hearing


A hearing allowed when a defendant makes a substantial preliminary showing that a warrant affidavit contained a deliberate or reckless falsehood needed for probable cause.

- **kind**: case-law
- **authority**: Franks v. Delaware, 438 U.S. 154 (1978) [existence-only]

### Eligible employee (FMLA)

- **definition**: An employee employed at least 12 months and at least 1,250 hours in the prior 12 months, at a worksite with 50 or more employees within 75 miles.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: 29 U.S.C. 2611(2), verbatim: "(A) In generalThe term “eligible employee” means an employee who has been employed— (i) for at least 12 months by the employer with respect to whom leave is requested under section 2612 of this title; and (ii) for at least 1,250 hours of service with such employer during the previous 12-month period."

### Serious health condition (FMLA)

- **definition**: An illness, injury, impairment or condition involving inpatient care or continuing treatment by a health care provider.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: 29 U.S.C. 2611(11), verbatim: "(11) Serious health conditionThe term “serious health condition” means an illness, injury, impairment, or physical or mental condition that involves— (A) inpatient care in a hospital, hospice, or residential medical care facility; or (B) continuing treatment by a health care provider."

### Qualified individual (ADA)

- **definition**: A person who, with or without reasonable accommodation, can perform the essential functions of the job.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: 42 U.S.C. 12111(8), verbatim: "The term “qualified individual” means an individual who, with or without reasonable accommodation, can perform the essential functions of the employment position that such individual holds or desires. For the purposes of this subchapter, consideration shall be given to the employer’s judgment as to what functions of a job are essential, and if an employer has prepared a written description before advertising or interviewing applicants for the job, this description shall be considered evidence of the essential functions of the job."

### Disability (ADA)

- **definition**: An impairment that substantially limits a major life activity, a record of one, or being regarded as having one.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: 42 U.S.C. 12102(1), verbatim: "(1) DisabilityThe term “disability” means, with respect to an individual— (A) a physical or mental impairment that substantially limits one or more major life activities of such individual; (B) a record of such an impairment; or (C) being regarded as having such an impairment (as described in paragraph (3))."

### Regarded as disabled


Being treated adversely because of an actual or perceived impairment, whether or not it limits a major life activity; does not cover impairments that are both transitory and minor (6 months or less).

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English summary of 42 U.S.C. 12102(3) (text read)

### Right-to-sue letter / notice

- **definition**: The agency notice that opens the 90-day window for filing suit (federal: 2000e-5(f)(1); Missouri: RSMo 213.111.1).
- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English summary of 42 U.S.C. 2000e-5(f)(1) and RSMo 213.111.1 (text read)

### Jurisdictional condition precedent (MHRA)

- **definition**: A requirement the Missouri statute itself calls jurisdictional: a verified complaint with the MCHR within 180 days of the discrimination.
- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English summary of RSMo 213.075.1 (text read)

### Worksharing / deferral agency


An arrangement under which a charge filed with the EEOC is treated as filed with the state agency (RSMo 213.075.2) and a longer federal filing period (300 days) can apply.

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English; RSMo 213.075.2 and 42 U.S.C. 2000e-5(e)(1) (text read); Missouri's agreement itself not verified

### Presumed father

- **definition**: A man the statute presumes to be the natural father based on marriage, attempted marriage, or a genetic test showing 98% or higher probability.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 210.822.1, verbatim: "1. A man shall be presumed to be the natural father of a child if:"

### Expert (paternity testing)

- **definition**: Includes a person who performs or analyzes an accredited genetic test performed by an approved laboratory.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 210.834.7, verbatim: "7. As used in sections 210.817 to 210.852, the term "expert" shall include, but not be limited to, a person who performs or analyzes a genetic test of a type generally acknowledged as reliable by accreditation bodies designated by the secretary of the Department of Health and Human Services pursuant to 42 U.S.C. Section 666(a) and performed by a laboratory approved by such accreditation bodies."

### Genetic testing in paternity

- **definition**: On any party's request the court shall order testing; refusal is contempt and can produce a default; a result excluding the man is conclusive of non-paternity.
- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English summary of RSMo 210.834 (text read)

### Public governmental body (Sunshine)

- **definition**: Any legislative, administrative or governmental entity, including departments of political subdivisions and committees of them.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 610.010(4), verbatim: "(4) "Public governmental body", any legislative, administrative or governmental entity created by the Constitution or statutes of this state, by order or ordinance of any political subdivision or district, judicial entities when operating in an administrative capacity, or by executive order, including:"

### Public record (Sunshine)

- **definition**: Any record, written or electronic, retained by or of a public governmental body.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 610.010(6), verbatim: "(6) "Public record", any record, whether written or electronically stored, retained by or of any public governmental body including any report, survey, memorandum, or other document or study prepared for the public governmental body by a consultant or other professional service paid for in whole or in part by public funds, including records created or maintained by private contractors under an agreement with a public governmental body or on behalf of a public governmental body; provided, however, that personally identifiable student records maintained by public educational institutions shall be open for inspection by the parents, guardian or other custodian of students under the age of eighteen years and by the parents, guardian or other custodian and the student if the student is over the age of eighteen years. The term "public record" shall not include any internal memorandum or letter received or prepared by or on behalf of a member of a public governmental body consisting of advice, opinions and recommendations in connection with the deliberative decision-making process of said body, unless such records are retained by the public governmental body or presented at a public meeting. Any document or study prepared for a public governmental body by a consultant or other professional service as described in this subdivision shall be retained by the public governmental body in the same manner as any other public record;"

### Closed record (Sunshine)

- **definition**: A record the body may withhold from the public under an exception.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 610.010(1), verbatim: "(1) "Closed meeting", "closed record", or "closed vote", any meeting, record or vote closed to the public;"

### Mobile video recording (law enforcement)

- **definition**: Data captured by a vehicle- or body-mounted device, including audio, video and metadata.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 610.100.1(7), verbatim: "(7) "Mobile video recording", any data captured by a mobile video recorder, including audio, video, and any metadata;"

### Expungement


A court order closing arrest/charge/conviction records so they are hidden from most people; two routes here, RSMo 610.122 (arrest record) and 610.140 (petition), each with exclusions and waiting periods.

- **kind**: general
- **authority**: plain-English summary of RSMo 610.122 and 610.140 (text read)

### Gross misconduct (POST)

- **definition**: Willful and wanton or unlawful conduct motivated by premeditated or intentional purpose or by purposeful indifference to consequences.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 590.080.1(1), verbatim: "(1) "Gross misconduct", includes any willful and wanton or unlawful conduct motivated by premeditated or intentional purpose or by purposeful indifference to the consequences of one's acts;"

### Moral turpitude (POST)

- **definition**: The wrongful quality shared by fraud, theft, bribery, illegal drug use, sexual misconduct and similar acts.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 590.080.1(2), verbatim: "(2) "Moral turpitude", the wrongful quality shared by acts of fraud, theft, bribery, illegal drug use, sexual misconduct, and other similar acts as defined by the common law of Missouri;"

### Reckless disregard (POST)

- **definition**: Conscious disregard of a substantial risk that amounts to a gross deviation from what a reasonable peace officer would do.
- **kind**: statutory
- **authority**: RSMo 590.080.1(3), verbatim: "(3) "Reckless disregard", a conscious disregard of a substantial risk that circumstances exist or that a result will follow, and such failure constitutes a gross deviation from the standard of care that a reasonable peace officer would exercise in the situation."


## encyclopedia

### Order of protection: petition, full order, renewal, notice, violation

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri (RSMo ch. 455)

An adult protective order starts with a verified petition and, often, a temporary ex parte order. A full order follows a hearing at which the respondent had notice and a chance to be heard. It normally lasts 180 days to one year (longer only with specific written findings), can be renewed on the petitioner's motion after a hearing, and may carry an automatic-renewal clause. Violating listed terms of an ex parte or full order is a crime.

- **elements**: Full order: petitioner proves abuse, stalking or sexual assault by a preponderance and respondent shows no legal justification (455.040.1(1)).; Renewal: motion plus hearing; no new act of abuse required (455.040.1(2)-(3)).; Violation (455.085.8): a valid order in effect, served or read to the defendant (or notice under 455.085.8(1)-(2)), and a violated listed term (Schleiermacher).
- **defenses**: No valid order in effect on the date (expired, vacated, void for want of notice).; No notice or service.; Charging-document defects and variance (order terms versus the charge).; Justification for the contact.
- **remedies**: Civil: custody, support, exclusion terms under 455.050. Criminal: class A misdemeanor, or class E felony with a prior plea or finding within five years (455.085.7-.8).
- **timeLimits**: Hearing within 15 days of the petition (455.040.1(1)); petition and hearing notice served at least three days before the hearing (455.040.2); appeal of the full order under the Supreme Court Rules.
- **pitfalls**: No statute states a notice period for a renewal hearing; '455.040.7-8' does not exist.; The last sentence of 455.040.2 concerns service of the ORDER, not notice of a hearing; quoting it alone misleads.; An auto-renewal clause may have renewed the order without any hearing; read the certified order.; 455.085 offenses cannot be expunged under 610.140.
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 455.010 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 455.040 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 455.085 [text read 2026-09-30]; State v. Schleiermacher, 924 S.W.2d 269 (Mo. banc 1996) [full-text]; State v. Chavez, 165 S.W.3d 545 (Mo. App. E.D. 2005) [full-text]; Ehrhart v. Ehrhart, 776 S.W.2d 450 (Mo. App. E.D. 1989) [existence-only] - annotation only; text is a stub
- **matters**: 26MR-CR00032; 26MR-CR00472; 25PI-PN00012 / ED114714

### Conversion

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

Conversion is the unauthorized assumption of ownership over another person's personal property, to the exclusion of the owner's rights. It can be shown three ways: a wrongful taking, a use or disposition that claims a right against the owner, or a refusal to return the property on demand. Possession that began lawfully, as with a tow yard, usually needs a demand and refusal unless the holder later disposes of the property as if it were its own.

- **elements**: Plaintiff owned or had the right to possess the property.; Defendant took it, used or disposed of it in opposition to the owner's rights, or refused a demand for return (Emerick).; Damages, typically the value when converted plus consequential loss.
- **defenses**: Plaintiff did not own or have the right to possession (the ownership fight in 26PI-CC00044/-53).; Consent or authority, including a good-faith belief based on a signed title.; A valid statutory lien (304.155.9) if the statute's notice steps were followed (Amond).
- **remedies**: Damages (value of the property, loss of use), punitive damages on proof of a culpable mental state, return of the specific property (replevin-type relief).
- **timeLimits**: Five years for taking, detaining or injuring chattels (RSMo 516.120(4)); the case file computes 12/8/2030 from the 12/8/2025 release.
- **pitfalls**: No Missouri case was located holding that a bailee delivering a vehicle to a non-owner is liable in conversion; that step is an inference.; Statements about who owned the Jeep made under oath in these civil cases can be used in the tampering case the State may refile (26RL-CR00078).
- **keyAuthorities**: Emerick v. Mutual Benefit Life Insurance Co., 756 S.W.2d 513 (Mo. banc 1988) [full-text]; Amond v. Ron York & Sons Towing, 302 S.W.3d 708 (Mo. App. E.D. 2009) [full-text]; RSMo 516.120 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 304.155 [text read 2026-09-30]
- **matters**: 26PI-CC00044; 26PI-CC00053; 26RL-CR00078

### Bailment

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri (general principles)

A bailment exists when an owner delivers personal property to someone for a limited purpose, and the holder must return it or deliver it as the owner directs. A towing or storage company that takes a vehicle is the natural bailee. The law database and case file assume the bailee owes at least reasonable care and must not release the property to someone without authority, but no controlling Missouri bailment case was verified in this project, so this article states general principles only.

- **elements**: Delivery of the property to the bailee and acceptance.; An express or implied agreement that it will be returned or delivered as directed.; Failure to return or wrongful delivery, causing loss.
- **defenses**: Delivery to the person the owner authorized.; Good-faith reliance on apparent ownership documents.; A statutory lien or sale procedure properly followed (304.155-304.157).; Comparative fault of the owner (Gustafson, general negligence).
- **remedies**: Damages equal to the property's value and loss of use; fees only if a statute or contract allows.
- **timeLimits**: Five years under RSMo 516.120(4) for injury to or detention of chattels (a tort route); contract-based theories are also five years under 516.120(1).
- **pitfalls**: Missouri authority on delivery to an impostor or non-owner was not located: flagged OPEN.; The 12/8/2025 release was day 4 after the wreck, before any five-day abandonment reporting trigger in 304.001(1).
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 304.001 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 304.155 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 304.156 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 516.120 [text read 2026-09-30]; Amond v. Ron York & Sons Towing, 302 S.W.3d 708 (Mo. App. E.D. 2009) [full-text] - conversion by towing company; Gustafson v. Benda, 661 S.W.2d 11 (Mo. banc 1983) [existence-only] - existence only
- **matters**: 26PI-CC00053

### Negligence per se

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

Negligence per se lets a plaintiff use violation of a statute or ordinance as proof that the defendant breached the duty of care. Missouri requires more than a violation: the plaintiff must be in the class the statute protects, the harm must be the kind it was designed to prevent, and the violation must have proximately caused the injury. Courts look to legislative intent and have declined to extend the doctrine to some economic or licensing statutes.

- **elements**: Violation of a statute or ordinance.; Injured party within the protected class.; Injury of the nature the statute was designed to prevent.; Violation was the proximate cause of the injury (Lowdermilk).
- **defenses**: The statute does not protect this class or this kind of harm.; No causation: the harm would have occurred anyway.; Excuse or compliance.
- **remedies**: Ordinary negligence damages; comparative fault reduces recovery.
- **timeLimits**: Follows the underlying claim: five years for injury to chattels under RSMo 516.120(4).
- **pitfalls**: For RSMo 304.156 the fit is weak: the notice statute protects redemption before title transfer, and the loss here flowed from the release, not a missing notice.; 304.156 contains no private right of action; better used as evidence of the standard of care and to rebut a lien defense.; Case-file Note mis-states who has the 15 working days: it is the Department of Revenue.
- **keyAuthorities**: Lowdermilk v. Vescovo Building & Realty Co., 91 S.W.3d 617 (Mo. App. E.D. 2002) [full-text]; Business Men's Assurance Co. of America v. Graham, 891 S.W.2d 438 (Mo. App. 1994) [existence-only] - existence only; cite through Lowdermilk; RSMo 304.156 [text read 2026-09-30]
- **matters**: 26PI-CC00053

### Civil conspiracy

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

A civil conspiracy is an agreement between two or more people to do an unlawful act or to use unlawful means to do a lawful act. It is not a stand-alone wrong: it adds liability (and sometimes reach to additional defendants) only when an underlying tort is proved. If the underlying tort claim fails, the conspiracy claim fails with it.

- **elements**: Two or more persons.; A common object, an unlawful act or unlawful means.; A meeting of the minds (agreement or understanding).; An unlawful act done in furtherance, causing damage (Oak Bluff).
- **defenses**: The underlying tort fails (for example, no conversion because Tyler cannot prove ownership).; No agreement; parallel but independent conduct.; Intracorporate and lawful-purpose arguments (general).
- **remedies**: Joint and several liability for the underlying tort damages; punitive damages if the underlying tort supports them.
- **timeLimits**: Follows the underlying tort (conversion five years; defamation and malicious prosecution two years, RSMo 516.140).
- **pitfalls**: In 26PI-CC00044 the conspiracy count rises or falls with conversion and fraud.; In 26MR-CV00879 the conspiracy count depends on defamation/false-report counts against two defendants; privilege issues apply to each.
- **keyAuthorities**: Oak Bluff Partners, Inc. v. Meyer, 3 S.W.3d 777 (Mo. banc 1999) (per curiam) [full-text]; RSMo 516.120 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 516.140 [text read 2026-09-30]
- **matters**: 26PI-CC00044; 26MR-CV00879

### Unjust enrichment

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

Unjust enrichment is an equitable claim to make a defendant give up a benefit it received and kept in circumstances where keeping it would be unfair. The benefit must come from the plaintiff to the defendant. It does not apply when the plaintiff received exactly what was bargained for or an express contract governs the same subject.

- **elements**: Plaintiff conferred a benefit on the defendant.; Defendant appreciated the benefit.; Defendant accepted and retained it under inequitable or unjust circumstances (Howard v. Turnbull).
- **defenses**: The benefit went to someone else (for example, to a company, not its owners).; Express contract covers the subject.; Voluntary payment or known-risk venture.; Retention is not unjust.
- **remedies**: Restitution of the benefit's value; constructive trust; no punitive damages.
- **timeLimits**: Generally five years (RSMo 516.120(1)); confirm for the specific theory.
- **pitfalls**: Tyler must show a benefit flowed to the Lake defendants from him; ownership of the Jeep is the predicate.; Unjust enrichment is a non-tort count: venue for it is set by RSMo 508.010.2 (defendant's county), not tort first-injury venue, so it cannot piggyback on tort counts (Burlison).
- **keyAuthorities**: Howard v. Turnbull, 316 S.W.3d 431 (Mo. App. W.D. 2010) [full-text]; State ex rel. Johnson & Johnson v. Burlison, 567 S.W.3d 168 (Mo. banc 2019) (SC96704, SC96710) [full-text]; RSMo 508.010 [text read 2026-09-30]
- **matters**: 26PI-CC00044

### Missouri Merchandising Practices Act (MMPA)

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri (RSMo 407.010-407.025)

The MMPA prohibits deception, fraud, false pretense, misrepresentation, unfair practices and concealment of material facts in connection with the sale or advertisement of merchandise, whether before, during or after the sale. A private plaintiff must have purchased or leased merchandise primarily for personal, family or household purposes and suffered an ascertainable loss as a result; the seller need not be the defendant, but the conduct must be connected to the plaintiff's own transaction.

- **elements**: Purchase or lease of merchandise for personal, family or household use.; An unlawful practice in connection with the sale (407.020).; Ascertainable loss of money or property as a result.; Reasonable-consumer conduct and definite, objective damages (407.025.1(2)).
- **defenses**: No purchase or lease by plaintiff.; Conduct not 'in connection with' the sale.; No reasonable consumer would have been misled.; No ascertainable loss.
- **remedies**: Actual damages, discretionary punitive damages, equitable relief and attorney's fees to the prevailing party, which can include the defendant (407.025.2).
- **timeLimits**: Accrues on purchase or lease or on notice of the practice (407.025.4); limitations period for MMPA claims was not verified.
- **pitfalls**: Count V in 26PI-CC00044 is the weakest: standing and a special venue rule (seller's or transaction's county) and fee exposure.; Not pleaded in 26PI-CC00053 (304.158.11's MMPA cross-reference belongs to 304.158, not 304.156).; The 2020 amendment postdates older annotations.
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 407.010 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 407.020 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 407.025 [text read 2026-09-30]; Conway v. CitiMortgage, Inc., 438 S.W.3d 410 (Mo. banc 2014) [full-text] - read in part; Ward v. West County Motor Co., 403 S.W.3d 82 (Mo. banc 2013) [existence-only] - existence only; Gibbons v. J. Nuckolls, Inc., 216 S.W.3d 667 (Mo. banc 2007) [existence-only] - existence only
- **matters**: 26PI-CC00044; 26PI-CC00053

### Defamation and qualified privilege

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

Defamation is publication to a third party of a false statement of fact that harms reputation. Missouri requires proof of actual damages in every defamation case. Reports to police made to help bring a criminal to justice are qualifiedly privileged, so a plaintiff must prove malice or bad faith; whether the privilege exists is decided by the court, but malice is a jury question if evidence of it is offered.

- **elements**: Publication of a defamatory statement.; The statement was false and concerned the plaintiff.; Fault of the defendant.; Actual damages (Nazeri).
- **defenses**: Truth or substantial truth.; Absolute or qualified privilege (reports to law enforcement), defeated only by malice.; Opinion rather than fact.
- **remedies**: Actual damages (reputation, emotional distress) and punitive damages on proof of malice; retraction is not addressed here.
- **timeLimits**: Two years (RSMo 516.140); the revisor annotation suggests accrual may depend on when damages are ascertained (annotation only).
- **pitfalls**: Case file cites Henry v. Halliburton for police-report privilege: wrong; use Rucker.; Brown is Witness 1 in 26MR-CR00032; civil demands must not touch the criminal case, and a conviction could have preclusive effect (James v. Paul, per case-file Note, unconfirmed).; Venue for defamation follows first publication (508.010.8).
- **keyAuthorities**: Nazeri v. Missouri Valley College, 860 S.W.2d 303 (Mo. banc 1993) [full-text]; Overcast v. Billings Mutual Insurance Co., 11 S.W.3d 62 (Mo. banc 2000) [full-text] - privilege is for the court; Rucker v. K-Mart Corp., 734 S.W.2d 533 (Mo. App. E.D. 1987) [full-text]; Highfill v. Hale, 186 S.W.3d 277 (Mo. banc 2006) [full-text] - false-imprisonment limit on reporters; RSMo 516.140 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 508.010 [text read 2026-09-30]
- **matters**: 26MR-CV00879

### False imprisonment

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

False imprisonment is confinement without legal justification. Those who directly procure, aid or assist an unlawful restraint can be liable, including a jailer who knows the arrest was illegal. A private person who merely reports a suspected crime is generally not liable when police independently observe facts and independently decide to arrest. The claim targets the detention itself (the arrest and jailing), not the later prosecution, so it can exist without a malicious-prosecution claim and the reverse.

- **elements**: Detention or restraint against the plaintiff's will.; Without legal justification.; Defendant caused, directed or participated in the confinement.
- **defenses**: Legal justification (valid warrant, probable cause).; Officer's independent decision breaks the causal chain (Highfill).; Statutory immunity for good-faith arrests under RSMo 455.085.4 for officers.
- **remedies**: Compensatory damages, punitive damages on proof of malice or conscious disregard.
- **timeLimits**: Two years (RSMo 516.140).
- **pitfalls**: Case-file Note: the 11/15 arrest involved video review and independent booking, weakening the theory against the reporter.; Counts VI-VII for false imprisonment are not yet pleaded in 26MR-CV00879; the one amendment as of course remains available (Rule 55.33(a)).; A detention under a facially valid warrant is usually justified unless the warrant was obtained by falsehood; no Missouri case on that point was read in this project.
- **keyAuthorities**: Rustici v. Weidemeyer, 673 S.W.2d 762 (Mo. banc 1984) [full-text]; Highfill v. Hale, 186 S.W.3d 277 (Mo. banc 2006) [full-text]; RSMo 516.140 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 455.085 [text read 2026-09-30]
- **matters**: 26MR-CV00879; 1983 pipeline (held; 4:26-cv unfiled)

### Malicious prosecution

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

Malicious prosecution compensates a person who was prosecuted without probable cause and with malice, where the prosecution ended in the person's favor. The defendant must have instigated the prosecution. Malice may be inferred from facts showing lack of probable cause but is not presumed as a matter of law. It differs from false imprisonment, which concerns the arrest or detention itself; this claim concerns the criminal case that followed, and either can exist without the other.

- **elements**: Commencement of a prosecution against the plaintiff.; Instigation by the defendant.; Termination in plaintiff's favor.; Want of probable cause.; Malice.; Damages (Sanders).
- **defenses**: Probable cause (including grand jury or prosecutor's independent decision).; Privileged report.; Not terminated in plaintiff's favor.
- **remedies**: Compensatory and punitive damages.
- **timeLimits**: Two years (RSMo 516.140); accrual on favorable termination (not researched here).
- **pitfalls**: A pending prosecution (26MR-CR00032) is not a termination in Tyler's favor; the claim cannot ripen until it ends favorably.; A dismissal without prejudice that the State may refile is a weak termination.; Malice is not presumed from lack of probable cause; it may be inferred from the facts that show the lack of probable cause.; Reporting a suspected crime is qualifiedly privileged; a plaintiff must overcome the privilege with evidence of bad faith.
- **keyAuthorities**: Sanders v. Daniel International Corp., 682 S.W.2d 803 (Mo. banc 1984) [full-text]; RSMo 516.140 [text read 2026-09-30]
- **matters**: 26MR-CV00879; 26RL-CR00078; 1983 pipeline (held; 4:26-cv unfiled)

### Tampering (RSMo 569.080 / 569.090)

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

First-degree tampering under 569.080.1(2) is a class D felony: knowingly receiving, possessing, selling or unlawfully operating a motor vehicle without the owner's consent. Second-degree tampering (569.090) covers tampering with another's property to cause substantial inconvenience, unlawfully riding in another's vehicle, and utility tampering. 'To tamper' means to interfere improperly, meddle, displace, alter, or temporarily deprive the owner.

- **elements**: 569.080.1(2): knowing receipt, possession, sale or unlawful operation of a motor vehicle.; Without the consent of the owner.; 569.090.1(1): tampers with property of another for the purpose of causing substantial inconvenience.
- **defenses**: The accused is the owner, or the owner consented (the case-file theory: Tyler holds title, registration, tax receipts).; Lack of knowledge.; Voluntary-act defense (562.011.3 for possession).
- **remedies**: Class D felony; 569.080.2 lets the court admit evidence of prior unlawful possession or of an acquisition far below value.
- **timeLimits**: Criminal limitations not researched here; the State says it will refile by about January 2027.
- **pitfalls**: Dismissed 8/26/2026 (prejudice status unconfirmed); testimony in civil cases is usable.; The HPD report paraphrases Tyler as saying he 'gifted' the Jeep (disputed) and Lake holds a signed title: ownership is contested.; 610.140 and 610.122 expungement interplay with a possible refiling.
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 569.080 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 569.090 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 569.010 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 562.011 [text read 2026-09-30]
- **matters**: 26RL-CR00078; 26RL-CV00280; 26PI-CC00044; 26PI-CC00053

### Resisting or interfering with arrest (RSMo 575.150)

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

Under the 2025 text, a person who knows or should know an officer is arresting or lawfully detaining someone and, to prevent it, resists by using or threatening violence or physical force or by fleeing commits the offense; interfering with another's arrest by force or physical interference is also covered. The officer's unlawful conduct is not a defense, but civil suits for unlawful arrest are preserved.

- **elements**: Knowledge or reason to know of an arrest, stop or detention.; Purpose of preventing it.; Violence, threatened violence or physical force, or fleeing (subdiv. 1).
- **defenses**: No purpose to prevent the arrest.; No voluntary act (562.011).; Conduct was not violence or physical force (no authority located for passive conduct).; No knowledge the person was an officer.
- **remedies**: Class A misdemeanor for non-felony arrests; class E felony for felony-warrant arrests and in fleeing cases creating substantial risk (575.150.5).
- **timeLimits**: Charged 1/27/2026; trial 11/4/2026.
- **pitfalls**: Unlawful arrest is not a defense to the criminal charge (575.150.4); raise it in a civil case.; Use the 2025 (H.B. 495) text, not the earlier wording.; A conviction may trigger the Heck bar in a later 1983 case.
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 575.150 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 562.011 [text read 2026-09-30]; State v. Shaw, No. SC97605 (Mo. banc Dec. 10, 2019); revisor gives 592 S.W.3d 354 [existence-only] - annotation; reporter cite unconfirmed
- **matters**: 26MR-CR00032; 1983 pipeline (held; 4:26-cv unfiled)

### Assault in the fourth degree (RSMo 565.056)

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

Fourth-degree assault is a class A misdemeanor covering attempting or recklessly causing physical injury, physical pain or illness (the version in force for 2026 offenses), criminally negligent firearm injury, purposely placing another in apprehension, reckless risk of death or serious injury, and offensive contact. Subdivisions (3) and (6) drop to class C unless the victim is a special victim such as a police officer on duty.

- **elements**: Subdivision (1): attempts to cause or recklessly causes physical injury, physical pain, or illness.; Mental state: attempt or recklessness.; Grading: class A; (3)/(6) class C unless special victim.
- **defenses**: No contact occurred, or contact was involuntary (562.011); these two theories are mutually exclusive.; Lack of recklessness.; Self-defense or force against excessive force by a known officer (MAI-CR 4th 406 series; numbers not verified).
- **remedies**: Class A misdemeanor sentence (authorized terms in RSMo 558.011, not verified here).
- **timeLimits**: Trial set 11/4/2026.
- **pitfalls**: The 7/1/2027 'bodily harm' version is what revisor.mo.gov displays by default; the pre-2027 text governs.; The warrant cites subdivision (5) (person with a disability), which does not fit an officer.; Do not commit to a physical account before Tyler decides D-1.
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 565.056 [pre-2027 version read from revisor all-versions page]; RSMo 565.002 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 562.011 [text read 2026-09-30]
- **matters**: 26MR-CR00032

### Speedy trial

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri and federal

Missouri's statute (RSMo 545.780) requires the court to set the case for trial as soon as reasonably possible after the defendant announces ready and files a request. It has no day count and does not authorize dismissal without a constitutional violation. The constitutional right is judged by the four Barker factors. Delay of eight months or more is reported as presumptively prejudicial, which triggers the inquiry only.

- **elements**: Statute: announcement of ready plus a request for a speedy trial.; Constitution: length of delay, reason, assertion of the right, prejudice (Barker).
- **defenses**: Delay caused or agreed to by the defense counts against the defendant.; Lack of prejudice.; Waiver by failure to assert.
- **remedies**: Mandamus to set the case; dismissal only with a constitutional violation (545.780.2).
- **timeLimits**: No fixed period. The 1/8/2027 and 2/21/2027 'dates' in the file trace to no rule; 2/21/2027 is a Sunday. Arrest 1/27/2026 to trial 11/4/2026 is 281 days.
- **pitfalls**: 'Rule 27.40(b)' does not exist.; 545.780 is not a dismissal vehicle.; McKee concerned a represented defendant's pro se filings; whether Tyler's 7/13/2026 demand announced ready is unverified.; Criminal time computes under Rule 20.01, not 44.01.
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 545.780 [text read 2026-09-30]; State ex rel. McKee v. Riley, 240 S.W.3d 720 (Mo. banc 2007) [full-text]; Barker v. Wingo, 407 U.S. 514 (1972) [existence-only] - existence only; State v. Williams, 34 S.W.3d 440 (Mo. App. S.D. 2001) [existence-only] - via McKee; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 20.01 [mirror text read]
- **matters**: 26MR-CR00032; 26MR-CR00472

### Venue and the 90-day deemed-granted rule

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

For a suit with a tort count where the plaintiff was first injured in Missouri, venue is the county of first injury, fixed as of the injury date; 'first injured' means where the trauma or exposure occurred. A motion to transfer or dismiss for improper venue is deemed granted if not denied within 90 days of filing unless all parties waive in writing, and the court then may only transfer. The rule applies to a defendant's motion.

- **elements**: Is there a tort count? Where was the plaintiff first injured (508.010.4, .9, .14)?; For non-tort counts, the defendant's county (508.010.2).; Timely motion under Rule 51.045(a) naming a county and its basis.
- **defenses**: Venue proper where filed (first-injury facts).; Motion procedurally deficient, so the 90 days never starts (ArchCity fn.3).; Waiver by an untimely motion.
- **remedies**: Transfer to a proper county; dismissal without prejudice only if no Missouri county is proper (508.010.15). Denial in error requires reversal without prejudice (508.010.16).
- **timeLimits**: Motion within 60 days of service; reply within 30 days of the motion; 90 days to rule. Lake motion day 90 = 10/25/2026; Griffin 10/27/2026; working target 10/23.
- **pitfalls**: The 90-day rule DOES run against Tyler in 26PI-CC00044.; Reply windows expired 8/26 and 8/28: leave for good cause is needed to add venue bases.; Unknown first-injury county.; Barron does not decide venue.
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 508.010 [text read 2026-09-30]; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 51.045 [mirror text read]; State ex rel. HeplerBroom, LLC v. Moriarty, 566 S.W.3d 240 (Mo. banc 2019) (No. SC97200) [full-text]; State ex rel. Universal Credit Acceptance, Inc. v. Reno, No. SC97872 (Mo. banc June 30, 2020) (reporter cite not located) [full-text]; State ex rel. ArchCity Defenders, Inc. v. Whyte, No. ED113019 (Mo. App. E.D. Jan. 21, 2025) [full-text]; State ex rel. Johnson & Johnson v. Burlison, 567 S.W.3d 168 (Mo. banc 2019) (SC96704, SC96710) [full-text]; State ex rel. Selimanovic v. Dierker, 246 S.W.3d 931 (Mo. banc 2008) [full-text]; State ex rel. Heartland Title Services, Inc. v. Harrell, 500 S.W.3d 239 (Mo. banc 2016) [full-text]
- **matters**: 26PI-CC00044

### Default judgment

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

If a defendant fails to plead or otherwise defend, the court may enter an interlocutory order of default and later a default judgment on proof of damages; a default judgment may also be entered without the interlocutory order. Missouri strongly prefers deciding cases on the merits, so a default is frequently set aside on a showing of a meritorious defense and good cause, which includes a mistake.

- **elements**: Service of process proved by the return.; Time to plead expired (30 days, Rule 55.25(a)).; Failure to plead or otherwise defend (Rule 74.05(a)-(b)).
- **defenses**: Not in default: an answer, motion, appearance or extension.; Service defects.; Set-aside: meritorious defense, good cause, reasonable time up to one year (74.05(d)).
- **remedies**: Default judgment with damages assessed by the court or, if demanded, a jury; set-aside may be conditioned on paying fees.
- **timeLimits**: Answer due 30 days after service; 26PI-CC00053: 10/1 (service 9/1) or 10/8/2026 (service 9/8).
- **pitfalls**: A default motion filed before the true date is the error that cannot be quietly cured.; The interlocutory order is 74.05(b) and set-aside is 74.05(d) (the file had them wrong).; In 26PI-CC00044 the default motion was withdrawn 9/11/2026.
- **keyAuthorities**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 74.05 [mirror text read]; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 55.25 [mirror text read]; Brungard v. Risky's Inc., 240 S.W.3d 685 (Mo. banc 2007) [full-text]; In re Marriage of Pierce, 867 S.W.2d 237 (Mo. App. 1993) [existence-only] - existence only
- **matters**: 26PI-CC00053; 26PI-CC00044

### Requests for admission

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri (Rule 59.01)

A party may serve up to 25 requests asking the other side to admit facts or document genuineness. Outside Chapter 517 cases, an unanswered request is deemed admitted, and admissions are conclusive unless the court allows withdrawal. The request must open with a bold capitalized warning and cannot be served on a defendant until 30 days after appearance or service of process; a defendant need not answer before 60 days.

- **elements**: Timely, valid request with the required warning and certificate of service.; Service on all parties not in default.; Failure to answer within the allowed time (30 days, or 60 days for a defendant).
- **defenses**: Request premature (invalid).; Form defects, over 25 requests.; Motion to withdraw or amend an admission.
- **remedies**: Deemed admissions conclusively established; sanctions under Rule 61.01(c).
- **timeLimits**: Response due 30 days after service; defendants at least 60 days after earlier of appearance or service of process (Rule 59.01(d)(1)).
- **pitfalls**: The database's 'RFA responses due ~Oct 1' is wrong; RFAs served with the petition are very likely void (Lane House).; Re-serve a compliant set after day 30; do not plan on deemed admissions.
- **keyAuthorities**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 59.01 [mirror text read]; Lane House Construction, Inc. v. Ogrowsky, 427 S.W.3d 882 (Mo. App. 2014) (district not confirmed) [full-text]; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 61.01 [mirror text read]
- **matters**: 26PI-CC00053

### Summary judgment

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

Summary judgment ends a claim without trial when the movant shows no genuine issue of material fact and a right to judgment as a matter of law. A genuine issue exists where the record has competent materials showing two plausible but contradictory accounts of essential facts. After a proper motion, the non-movant must answer with specific facts, not pleadings.

- **elements**: Movant's statement of uncontroverted facts supported by record citations.; No genuine dispute on facts essential to the claim or a defense.; Legal entitlement to judgment.
- **defenses**: A genuine dispute exists (competing affidavits, documents).; Movant failed to meet its own burden.; Inferences run for the non-movant.
- **remedies**: Judgment for the movant; partial summary judgment on issues or counts.
- **timeLimits**: Motion timing and response periods are set by Rule 74.04 (current text not read in this project).
- **pitfalls**: ITT is a 1993 opinion and Rule 74.04 has been amended since; read the current rule.; Affidavits that contradict prior sworn testimony do not create a genuine issue (ITT).
- **keyAuthorities**: ITT Commercial Finance Corp. v. Mid-America Marine Supply Corp., 854 S.W.2d 371 (Mo. banc 1993) [full-text]
- **matters**: 26PI-CC00044; 26PI-CC00053; 26MR-CV00879

### Appellate record and briefs

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri (Rules 81 and 84)

The record on appeal is the legal file plus the transcript, prepared by the appellant and filed with the appellate clerk within 30 days of the notice of appeal (legal file only) or 90 days (when a transcript is needed). The appellate court may enlarge that time on application. Briefs follow: appellant 60 days after the record, respondent 30 days after that, reply 15 days later, each in the form prescribed by Rule 84.04.

- **elements**: Order the transcript in writing within ten days and file the order/payment certificate (Rule 81.12(c)(1)).; File the record by the Rule 81.19 date or get an enlargement (Rule 81.20).; Points relied on in the prescribed form, with record cites and a preservation statement.
- **defenses**: Good cause for delay (transcript not complete; IFP timing).; Excusable neglect if the request comes after expiry (Rule 44.01(b)(2)).
- **remedies**: Extension of time; if steps are not taken the appellate court may dismiss (RSMo 512.050).
- **timeLimits**: ED114714: 7/9/2026 + 90 = 10/7/2026 (alternatives 10/22 or 10/28 if the notice is treated as filed on 7/24 or 7/30); brief 60 days after the record is actually filed.
- **pitfalls**: An audio CD is not a Rule 81.12(c) transcript.; Service on a protected party could be a prohibited contact.; Rule 81.14 is repealed; the extension vehicle is Rule 81.20 in the Court of Appeals.; Eastern District local rules (310, 330, 340) must be confirmed.
- **keyAuthorities**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.12 [mirror text read]; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.19 [mirror text read]; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.20 [mirror text read]; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 84.04 [mirror text read]; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 84.05 [mirror text read]; RSMo 512.050 [text read 2026-09-30]
- **matters**: 25PI-PN00012 / ED114714

### Standard of review

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

In a court-tried civil case, including an order-of-protection appeal, the judgment is affirmed unless there is no substantial evidence to support it, it is against the weight of the evidence, or it erroneously declares or applies the law. Weight-of-evidence reversals are used cautiously and only with a firm belief the judgment is wrong. The appellate court gives due regard to the trial judge's view of witness credibility. Continuance rulings are reviewed for abuse of discretion.

- **elements**: Identify the ruling and its standard (law: independent review; fact: deference).; Show the error was preserved, or request plain error (Rule 84.13(c)).
- **defenses**: Non-preservation.; Harmless error (84.13(b): must affect the merits).; Credibility deference.
- **remedies**: Affirmance, reversal, remand, or modification.
- **timeLimits**: Follows Rules 84.04-84.05.
- **pitfalls**: Continuance denial: without a written motion and affidavit under Rule 65.03 there is no abuse of discretion (S.J.H.; adverse to ED114714 Point III).; 'Not briefed or not properly briefed' is unreviewable (84.13(a)).; Murphy construes former Rule 73.01; cite 84.13(d) as the current rule.
- **keyAuthorities**: Murphy v. Carron, 536 S.W.2d 30 (Mo. banc 1976) [full-text]; S.J.H. v. J.P.H., No. ED112072 (Mo. App. E.D. Oct. 1, 2024) [full-text]; L.E.C. v. K.R.C., No. ED110515 (Mo. App. E.D. June 20, 2023) [existence-only] - existence only; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 84.13 [mirror text read]; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 65.03 [mirror text read]
- **matters**: 25PI-PN00012 / ED114714

### Section 1983 (Heck, Monell, Pembaur, Imbler, Franks, qualified immunity)

- **jurisdiction**: Federal

Section 1983 provides damages against people who, acting under color of state law, violate federal rights. Claims against a county or city require an official policy or custom (Monell), which a single decision by a final policymaker can supply (Pembaur). Prosecutors have absolute immunity for charging decisions (Imbler); officers have qualified immunity unless they violated clearly established law (Harlow, Pearson). A claim that would necessarily show a standing conviction invalid is barred (Heck). Franks allows challenges to false warrant statements.

- **elements**: Person acting under color of state law.; Deprivation of a constitutional or federal right.; Causation and damages; for local governments, policy or custom as the moving force.
- **defenses**: Absolute and qualified immunity.; Heck bar if a conviction stands.; Statute of limitations: five years in Missouri (Sulik).; Probable cause or independent intervening decision.
- **remedies**: Compensatory and, against individuals, punitive damages; attorney's fees for prevailing plaintiffs under 42 U.S.C. 1988 (not read here); injunctive relief.
- **timeLimits**: Five years (RSMo 516.120(4), via Sulik); false-arrest claims accrue when detention under legal process begins (Wallace).
- **pitfalls**: All pipeline matters are HELD and unfiled; nothing has been sent.; Heck speaks of expungement by executive order, not a records expungement.; Commissioner Lake's Monell theory depends on policymaking authority under Missouri law (not verified).; Sworn statements here can be used in the criminal cases.
- **keyAuthorities**: 42 U.S.C. 1983 [LII text read]; Heck v. Humphrey, 512 U.S. 477 (1994) [existence-only] - existence only; Wallace v. Kato, 549 U.S. 384 (2007) [existence-only] - existence only; Monell v. New York City Department of Social Services, 436 U.S. 658 (1978) [existence-only] - existence only; Pembaur v. City of Cincinnati, 475 U.S. 469 (1986) [existence-only] - existence only; Imbler v. Pachtman, 424 U.S. 409 (1976) [existence-only] - existence only; Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800 (1982) [existence-only] - existence only; Franks v. Delaware, 438 U.S. 154 (1978) [existence-only] - existence only; Sulik v. Taney County, 393 F.3d 765 (8th Cir. 2005) [full-text]
- **matters**: 1983 pipeline (held; 4:26-cv unfiled); 26MR-CR00032; 26MR-CR00472; AG/DOJ/POST complaints

### FMLA, ADA and MHRA employment claims

- **jurisdiction**: Federal and Missouri

Three regimes can apply to one termination. The FMLA protects eligible employees who take leave for a serious health condition from interference and retaliation and lets them sue directly in court. The ADA bars disability discrimination and requires an EEOC charge first. The Missouri Human Rights Act bars disability discrimination but requires a verified MCHR complaint within 180 days, which it calls jurisdictional; an EEOC charge is deemed filed with the MCHR on the EEOC receipt date.

- **elements**: FMLA: eligible employee, qualifying leave, interference or retaliation, prejudice.; ADA: covered employer, disability, qualified individual, adverse action because of disability.; MHRA: protected classification was the direct proximate cause of the decision (213.111.5).
- **defenses**: Legitimate reason (here 'dishonesty' work-rule violation).; Not eligible or not covered.; Untimely charge (MHRA is jurisdictional).; Would have been discharged anyway.
- **remedies**: FMLA: lost wages, interest, equal liquidated damages, reinstatement, fees. MHRA: actual and punitive damages capped by employer size, fees, jury trial. ADA: Title VII remedies.
- **timeLimits**: MHRA 180 days: 11/5/2026 (from 5/9/2026). EEOC 180 days 11/5/2026, or 300 days 3/5/2027 if deferral applies. Suit within 90 days of a right-to-sue notice. FMLA 2 years (3 willful): 5/9/2028/2029.
- **pitfalls**: Do not cite Huber v. Westar Foods, 106 F.4th 725 (vacated); the en banc decision is 139 F.4th 615.; Confirm whether the EEOC charge (No. 560-2026-03085) was actually filed and when.; MHRA revisor annotations predate the 2017 amendments.
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 213.055 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 213.075 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 213.111 [text read 2026-09-30]; 29 U.S.C. 2615 [LII text read]; 29 U.S.C. 2617 [LII text read]; 42 U.S.C. 12112 [LII text read]; 42 U.S.C. 2000e-5(e)(1) [LII text read]; Huber v. Westar Foods, Inc., 139 F.4th 615 (8th Cir. 2025) (en banc) [existence-only] - existence-only
- **matters**: EEOC 560-2026-03085

### Expungement

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri

Missouri allows court orders closing arrest and court records through two main routes: RSMo 610.122 for arrest records (false-information arrests and certain traffic dismissals) and RSMo 610.140, a petition route with waiting periods, exclusions, lifetime limits and a public-welfare finding. An expunged record is closed and the person may generally answer 'no' to employer inquiries, with listed exceptions.

- **elements**: 610.140: eligible crime, petition contents, waiting period (misdemeanor one year after the disposition; arrest-only petition 18 months), no pending charges, conduct not a threat, public-welfare finding.; 610.122(1): false-information arrest, no probable cause now, no charges will be pursued, no SIS.; No pending civil action relating to the arrest or records (610.122.2).
- **defenses**: Prosecutor objection within 30 days and rebuttal of the presumption.; Excluded offense (for example 455.085).; Pending charges.
- **remedies**: Closure of records; restoration of civil rights as if the events did not occur, with listed exceptions.
- **timeLimits**: Court rules within six months of a 610.140 petition; waiting periods as above; lifetime limits of three misdemeanors and two felonies.
- **pitfalls**: The 26RL-CV00280 Matter cites 'ch. 614/614.100' and '543.018': do not match the statutes read.; The State's planned refiling defeats 'no charges will be pursued'.; Expungement is not Heck favorable termination.
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 610.122 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 610.140 [text read 2026-09-30]; In re Dyer, 163 S.W.3d 915 (Mo. banc 2005) [existence-only] - annotation; existence only
- **matters**: 26RL-CV00280; 26RL-CR00078

### Paternity and genetic testing

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri (RSMo 210.817-210.852)

A paternity action can be brought by the child, mother, a presumed father, a man alleging himself to be the father, a custodian of over 60 days, or the family support division. A presumption arises from marriage, attempted marriage, acknowledgment or a 98% genetic test, and is rebutted only by clear and convincing evidence. On any party's request the court must order genetic tests; an excluding result is conclusive of non-paternity.

- **elements**: Standing under 210.826.; Presumption or no presumption (210.822).; Testing request and expert report (210.834).
- **defenses**: Acknowledgment of paternity not timely rescinded (210.823).; Refusal to test (contempt; default).; Challenge to testing procedure filed 30+ days before trial.
- **remedies**: Declaration of the father-child relationship or of non-paternity; support and custody follow under chapter 452.
- **timeLimits**: Subsection 1 actions 'at any time'; acknowledgments rescindable within 60 days.
- **pitfalls**: Case-file cites 210.826.2 for 'at any time': those words are in subsection 1.; Docket is confidential; Griffin's statement that she is 'certain' Tyler is not the father is from AI-transcribed audio.; Custody in the protective order (455.050) interacts with parentage.
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 210.822 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 210.823 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 210.826 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 210.830 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 210.834 [text read 2026-09-30]; State ex rel. Newton v. Conklin, 767 S.W.2d 112 (Mo. App. S.D. 1989) [existence-only] - annotation; existence only
- **matters**: 26PI-FC00048; 25PI-PN00012 / ED114714

### Sunshine Law (public records)

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri (RSMo 610.010-610.200)

The Sunshine Law makes meetings and records of public governmental bodies open unless an exception applies; exceptions are strictly construed. Custodians must act on a request within three business days. Law enforcement incident and arrest reports are open; mobile video recordings and investigative reports are closed until the investigation is inactive, with exceptions for people depicted and a court-action route. Violations can bring penalties and fees.

- **elements**: A public governmental body and a public record (610.010).; A request to the custodian.; Denial, delay or unjustified closure.
- **defenses**: Closed-record exceptions (for example 610.100.2(2) for active investigations).; Reasonable cause for delay beyond three days.
- **remedies**: Court order for disclosure; civil penalty up to $1,000 (knowing) or $5,000 (purposeful) and fees (610.027).
- **timeLimits**: Response within three business days (610.023.3); one year from ascertainable violation for voiding closed-meeting actions, never more than two years (610.027.5).
- **pitfalls**: The HPD 7/16/2026 Sunshine letter status is unknown.; Bodycam closure status depends on whether the investigation is inactive; the criminal discovery route (Rule 25.03) is separate.; Only 610.100 subsections 1-4 were read.
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 610.010 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 610.011 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 610.023 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 610.027 [text read 2026-09-30]; RSMo 610.100 [text read 2026-09-30]; McLachlan v. McNary, 684 S.W.2d 534 (Mo. App. 1984) [existence-only] - annotation; existence only
- **matters**: 26MR-CR00032; 26MR-CR00472; AG/DOJ/POST complaints

### Evidence at a Missouri trial: foundations, hearsay exceptions and video

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri (statutes + case law; no codified evidence code)

Missouri has no single evidence code; the rules come from statutes in chapters 490–491 and case law. For your trial the key questions are how the State (or you) lays a foundation for video and records, when out-of-court statements come in, and how to preserve objections.

- **elements**: Video foundation: a witness to the events says it is fair and accurate (McNear), OR the ‘silent witness’ showing — system working, reliable and tamper-protected, copy matches original (Moyle); Business records: custodian/qualified witness + regular course + near the time + trustworthiness (RSMo 490.680), or by affidavit (RSMo 490.692); Prior inconsistent statements in criminal cases: RSMo 491.074; Impeachment by prior conviction: RSMo 491.050; Forfeiture by wrongdoing: RSMo 491.016 (hearing outside the jury; preponderance); Experts: RSMo 490.065 subsection 2 (Daubert-style) in criminal jury trials; subsection 1 in ch. 455 hearings
- **defenses**: Object to foundation if no witness saw the events and the State can’t show system reliability and an unaltered copy; Demand the original-format files and metadata (Rule 25.03) to test the ‘copy matches original’ factor; Object under 491.016 unless the State proves intent to keep the witness away
- **remedies**: Exclusion; limiting instruction (MAI-CR 4th 410.16 / 410.10); plain-error review only if not preserved.
