- **timeLimits**: Motions in limine: 5 days before trial in Pike (45th L.R. 33.4); all motions before trial in Marion (10th L.R. 67.7). Objection at trial + motion for new trial within 15 days (Rule 29.11).
- **pitfalls**: A ruling in limine preserves nothing — object again when the evidence is offered; Leaving an objection out of the motion for new trial = plain error only (Whittier); Footage recorded in a nonpublic location: RSMo 610.100.8 requires notice to depicted persons and a 10-day wait before you display or describe it; Contact with witnesses can open the door to 491.016
- **keyAuthorities**: RSMo 490.065; RSMo 490.680; RSMo 490.692; RSMo 491.016; RSMo 491.050; RSMo 491.074; RSMo 610.100; State v. Moyle, 532 S.W.3d 733 (Mo. App. W.D. 2017); State v. McNear, 343 S.W.3d 703 (Mo. App. S.D. 2011); State v. Whittier (Mo. App. 2019)
- **matters**: 26MR-CR00032; 26MR-CR00472

### Jury instructions in a Missouri criminal trial: getting, drafting and preserving them

- **jurisdiction**: Missouri (Rules 28.02, 28.03, 29.11; MAI-CR 4th; 10th Cir. L.R. 53.1)

MAI-CR 4th forms are mandatory where they apply. The verdict director for each count lists what the State must prove; the defense adds converse, alibi and limiting instructions on request. Errors are lost unless objected to specifically before the jury retires and repeated in the motion for new trial.

- **elements**: Identify each count’s verdict director: 432.06 (order of protection), 429.60 or 429.61 (resisting), 419.16/419.18 (assault 4th); Request one converse (408.02) per verdict director, aimed at your strongest element; Request alibi (408.06) if supported, with time/place specificity (404.02 NOU 5–6); Request limiting instructions (410.16/410.10) for prior-act or impeachment evidence; Mark every request ‘MAI-CR ___’, ‘Modified’ or ‘Not in MAI-CR’; original + copy for the court + copy per party
- **defenses**: Variance between the information and the verdict director (e.g., place vs. person in Count 1); Wrong resisting instruction (arrest vs. stop); Multiple-acts unanimity problem (Celis-Garcia)
- **remedies**: New trial or reversal if the error was preserved and prejudicial; plain error otherwise.
- **timeLimits**: Marion County: proposed instructions due AT THE PRETRIAL CONFERENCE (10th L.R. 53.1). Objections before the jury retires (Rule 28.03). Motion for new trial within 15 days of verdict (Rule 29.11).
- **pitfalls**: The texts of 432.06, 419.16/419.18 and the non-flight 429.60 form could not be retrieved here — get them from the clerk or the State’s proposed set; Refused instructions must be marked and filed to be in the record; Don’t converse an affirmative defense
- **keyAuthorities**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 28.02; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 28.03; Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.11; MAI-CR 4th 408.02, 408.06, 410.10, 410.16, 404.02; State v. Steward (Mo. App. 2020); State v. Celis-Garcia, 344 S.W.3d 150 (Mo. banc 2011)
- **matters**: 26MR-CR00032; 26MR-CR00472

### State ex rel. HeplerBroom, LLC v. Moriarty

- **cite**: 566 S.W.3d 240 (Mo. banc 2019) (No. SC97200)
- **court**: Mo. Sup. Ct. (en banc)
- **year**: 2019

Section 508.010.10 applies to a defendant's motion to transfer for improper venue: when the circuit court did not rule within 90 days and no written waiver by all parties existed, the motion was deemed granted and the court lacked authority to do anything but transfer; writ of prohibition made permanent.

- **statutes**: RSMo 508.010; Rule 51.045
- **verification**: full-text
- **treatment**: Quoted and followed in State ex rel. Universal Credit Acceptance v. Reno (Mo. banc 2020) (read in this task; pin 244 confirmed). Cited by 6 on CourtListener; not a citator check. | Negative-treatment screen 2026-10-06: CourtListener search of later citing opinions for ‘overruled / abrogated / disapproved / superseded’ found none. This is a screen, not a citator.
- **caution**: Pin cite 244 for the holding sentence was taken from Reno's parenthetical; CourtListener text lacks page numbers for that page. Verbatim sentence confirmed: 'the plain language of section 508.010.10 requires this Court to hold the circuit court's failure to rule upon Relators' motion to transfer within the ninety-day period resulted in Relators' motion being deemed granted.'

### State ex rel. Heartland Title Services, Inc. v. Harrell

- **cite**: 500 S.W.3d 239 (Mo. banc 2016)
- **court**: Mo. Sup. Ct. (en banc)
- **year**: 2016

Each count must pass venue muster (n.4); Missouri has no intrastate forum non conveniens (n.6); when venue is improper the court has a ministerial duty to transfer. Revisor annotation to 508.010: if personal and subject-matter jurisdiction exist, venue is proper in any county absent an express restriction.

### Chromalloy American Corp. v. Elyria Foundry Co.

- **cite**: 955 S.W.2d 1 (Mo. banc 1997)
- **court**: Mo. Sup. Ct. (en banc)
- **year**: 1997

"The general rule is that a dismissal without prejudice is not a final judgment and, therefore, is not appealable." Exception: a dismissal that effectively precludes refiling. "A dismissal without prejudice that a plaintiff may cure by filing another suit in the same court is not a final judgment from which an appeal may be taken."

- **statutes**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 67.03
- **verification**: full-text
- **treatment**: Passages read 2026-10-05 in the CourtListener opinion text (opinion 2449900). No citator check.
- **caution**: Check later cases before citing.

### State v. Moyle

- **cite**: 532 S.W.3d 733 (Mo. App. W.D. 2017)
- **court**: Mo. App. W.D.
- **year**: 2017

Video may be authenticated by a witness who saw the events and says it is fair and accurate, or under the ‘silent witness’ theory by showing (1) the system was working properly, (2) its historic reliability and protection from tampering, and (3) the trial copy fairly and accurately reflects the original or any change is explained.

- **statutes**: RSMo 490.680
- **verification**: full-text
- **treatment**: Passages read this session (CourtListener); followed in State v. Whittier (Mo. App. 2019).
- **caution**: Citator not run.

- **cite**: 343 S.W.3d 703, 704 (Mo. App. S.D. 2011)
- **court**: Mo. App. S.D.
- **year**: 2011

A victim who witnessed the event can lay the foundation for surveillance video by testifying it is a fair and accurate recording, even without knowing how the system works.

- **verification**: existence-only
- **treatment**: Proposition as quoted in State v. Moyle; McNear itself not read.
- **caution**: Read before citing.

- **cite**: (Mo. App. Oct. 1, 2019) (CourtListener opinion 4443095; district and reporter cite not confirmed)

- **verification**: full-text

- **statutes**: RSMo 575.150
- **verification**: full-text

### State v. Bax

- **cite**: (Mo. App. Apr. 28, 2015) (CourtListener cluster 2823092; Cole County case; reporter cite not confirmed)

Unobjected instructional error is reviewed only for plain error under Rule 30.20; giving an unneeded optional definition violated Rule 28.02 but caused no manifest injustice.

## latin

### ab initio

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: from the beginning
- **meaning**: From the start; e.g. void ab initio — never valid at all, rather than becoming invalid later.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/ab_initio

### abandonment

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Voluntarily giving up property or a right with intent not to reclaim it.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English term, not Latin.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/abandonment

### accession

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: addition
- **meaning**: Acquiring title to additions or improvements to property.
- **status**: reviewed
- **note**: English term, not Latin.
- **origin**: English

### accessory (before / after the fact)

- **sections**: Criminal
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Common-law categories: one who helps before the crime (accessory before) or shields the offender afterward (accessory after).
- **status**: reviewed
- **note**: English terms, not Latin.
- **origin**: English
- **mo**: Missouri replaced these categories: responsibility for the conduct of another, RSMo 562.041; after-the-fact help is hindering prosecution, RSMo 575.030 (text read on revisor.mo.gov 2026-10-06).

### actus curiae neminem gravabit

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: an act of the court shall prejudice no one
- **meaning**: A party should not suffer for the court’s own error or delay.
- **status**: reviewed
- **origin**: Latin

### actus Dei nemini facit injuriam

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: an act of God does wrong to no one
- **meaning**: No one is liable for loss caused purely by natural forces.
- **status**: reviewed
- **origin**: Latin

### actus legis nemini facit injuriam

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: the act of the law does wrong to no one
- **meaning**: Operation of law itself creates no liability.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: “Change in law doesn’t create liability” narrows it.
- **origin**: Latin

### actus non facit reum nisi mens sit rea

- **sections**: Criminal; Maxims
- **literal**: an act does not make one guilty unless the mind is guilty
- **meaning**: Guilt requires both a guilty act and a guilty mind.
- **status**: reviewed
- **origin**: Latin

### actus reus

- **sections**: Procedural; Criminal
- **literal**: guilty act
- **meaning**: The physical element of a crime: a voluntary act, or a failure to act when the law imposes a duty. Reflexes and other involuntary movements do not count.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Missouri: RSMo 562.011 (voluntary act) — see Definitions tab, “Voluntary act”.
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/actus_reus

### ad damnum

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: to the damage / in accordance with the loss
- **meaning**: The clause of a civil petition or complaint stating the money damages claimed.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: RSMo 509.050: a petition demanding money may state no dollar amount except to establish jurisdiction; it prays for damages “fair and reasonable” (text read on revisor.mo.gov 2026-10-06).
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/ad%20damnum

### ad hoc

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: for this (purpose)
- **meaning**: Formed for one particular purpose or problem, not general or permanent.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/ad%20hoc

### ad hominem

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: to the person
- **meaning**: An argument that attacks the opponent personally instead of answering the point made.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/ad%20hominem

### ad litem

- **sections**: Procedural; Family
- **literal**: for the suit
- **meaning**: Appointed only for the purposes of a lawsuit, e.g. a guardian ad litem or attorney ad litem.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/ad_litem

### ad valorem

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: according to the value
- **meaning**: A tax or duty measured by the assessed value of the property.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/ad_valorem

### addendum

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: thing to be added
- **meaning**: A supplement to a document.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/addendum

### adjoining

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Touching or next to.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English term, not Latin.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/adjoining

### admissum

- **sections**: Evidence
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Not a recognized legal term. Use “admitted (in evidence)”.
- **status**: not-recognized
- **note**: Remove from use.
- **origin**: Latin

### adverse possession

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Acquiring title by open, hostile, continuous possession for the statutory period.
- **status**: reviewed
- **note**: English term, not Latin.
- **origin**: English

### affidavit

- **sections**: Evidence
- **literal**: he has declared on oath
- **meaning**: A written statement of fact sworn or affirmed before an officer authorized to administer oaths.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/affidavit

### affiliation (proceeding)

- **sections**: Family
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: A proceeding to establish paternity.
- **status**: reviewed
- **note**: English term.
- **origin**: English
- **mo**: Missouri Uniform Parentage Act: RSMo 210.817-.852 (see Statutes tab).

### alias

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: otherwise (called); at another time

(1) Another name a person uses (from alias dictus, “otherwise called”). (2) An alias writ or summons is a second one issued after the first was not served or produced no result.

- **status**: corrected
- **note**: “Second” is not a translation; the Latin means “otherwise”. The second-writ meaning is correct but is only one use.
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Missouri issues alias summonses when the original is not served — check your circuit clerk’s practice.
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/alias; https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/alias

### alibi

- **sections**: Procedural; Criminal
- **literal**: elsewhere

Evidence that the accused was somewhere else when the crime happened. It is not an affirmative defense: the State keeps the burden of proving presence beyond a reasonable doubt.

- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: Added: the burden stays on the prosecution.
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Missouri: on the State’s written request the defense must disclose the alibi place and witnesses (Rule 25.05, read on the rules mirror 2026-10-06). Instruction: MAI-CR 4th 408.06 (see Statutes tab).
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/alibi

### alimony

- **sections**: Family
- **literal**: nourishment
- **meaning**: Spousal support.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English word of Latin origin.
- **origin**: English
- **mo**: Missouri calls it “maintenance”: RSMo 452.335 (text read on revisor.mo.gov 2026-10-06).
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/alimony

### allegatum

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Not a recognized legal term. Use “allegation”.
- **status**: not-recognized
- **note**: Remove from use.
- **origin**: Latin

### amicus curiae

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: friend of the court
- **meaning**: A non-party, usually with a strong interest in the issue, that files a brief with the court’s permission. Plural: amici curiae.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/amicus_curiae; https://www.uscourts.gov/glossary

### animus furandi

- **sections**: Criminal
- **literal**: intent to steal
- **meaning**: The intent to permanently deprive required for theft.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Missouri stealing (purpose to deprive): RSMo 570.030 (text read on revisor.mo.gov 2026-10-06).
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/animus%20furandi

### animus injuriandi

- **sections**: Criminal
- **literal**: intent to injure
- **meaning**: Intent to harm or insult (chiefly a civil-law defamation concept).
- **status**: reviewed
- **origin**: Latin

### appendix

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: an appendage
- **meaning**: Supplementary material at the end of a document or brief.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/appendix

### appurtenance

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: belonging to
- **meaning**: A right attached to land that passes with it.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English term, not Latin.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/appurtenance

### attestation

- **sections**: Evidence
- **literal**: witnessing (from Latin attestari)
- **meaning**: The act of witnessing a signature or document and signing as a witness.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English word of Latin origin, not a Latin term.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/attestation

### audi alteram partem

- **sections**: Procedural; Maxims
- **literal**: hear the other side
- **meaning**: Natural-justice rule that no one should be judged without a fair chance to be heard; the core of procedural due process (notice and an opportunity to be heard).
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/audi%20alteram%20partem

### autrefois acquit / autrefois convict

- **sections**: Criminal
- **literal**: formerly acquitted / formerly convicted
- **meaning**: Pleas in bar that the defendant was already tried for the same offense (double jeopardy).
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: Law French.
- **origin**: Law French
- **mo**: U.S. Const. amend. V; Mo. Const. art. I, § 19.
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/autrefois%20acquit

### bona fide

- **literal**: in good faith
- **meaning**: Genuine; without fraud.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/bona%20fide

### complaint / petition

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: The initial pleading in a civil case.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: “Expression of grief” is etymology, not meaning.
- **origin**: English
- **mo**: Missouri calls it a petition and requires fact pleading (Rule 55.05).

### compos mentis

- **sections**: Criminal
- **literal**: of sound mind
- **meaning**: Mentally competent.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/compos%20mentis

### concur

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: agree
- **meaning**: A judge’s agreement with the majority. A judge who agrees with the result but not the reasoning “concurs in the judgment” (or specially).
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: “Agrees with result but not reasoning” describes only a special concurrence.
- **origin**: English

### concurrence

- **sections**: Criminal; Appellate & writs
- **literal**: a running together
- **meaning**: (1) Criminal law: the act and the required mental state must occur together. (2) Appellate: a judge’s separate opinion agreeing with the result.
- **status**: source-checked

English word of Latin origin. Dictionary confirms the appellate sense; the criminal-law sense (union of act and intent) is standard usage but was not in the entry fetched.

- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/concurrence

### condominium

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: joint ownership
- **meaning**: Individual ownership of units plus shared ownership of common areas.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/condominium

### consanguinity

- **sections**: Family
- **literal**: blood relationship
- **meaning**: Kinship by blood (vs. affinity, by marriage).
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/consanguinity

### consortium

- **sections**: Family
- **literal**: partnership; fellowship
- **meaning**: The companionship and services of a spouse; loss of consortium is a derivative claim.
- **status**: reviewed
- **origin**: Latin

### constructive possession

- **sections**: Criminal
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Knowing power and intention to control a thing not in physical custody.
- **status**: reviewed
- **note**: English term.
- **origin**: English
- **mo**: RSMo 556.061(38): “possess” includes actual or constructive possession with knowledge of the object’s presence (text read on revisor.mo.gov 2026-10-06); version effective 8/28/2026 — check the version for your offense date.

### contemporanea expositio est optima et fortissima in lege

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: contemporaneous exposition is the best and strongest in law
- **meaning**: Interpret a text as it was understood when made.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: Spelling: expositio, not “exposition”.
- **origin**: Latin

### contiguous

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: touching
- **meaning**: Sharing a boundary.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English term, not Latin.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/contiguous

### conveyance

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: A transfer of an interest in land, usually by deed.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English term, not Latin.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/conveyance

### coram nobis

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: before us (in our presence)
- **meaning**: Writ asking the same court that entered judgment to correct it for an error of fact that was not in the record and would have prevented the judgment.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: Clarified: it fixes errors of fact outside the record, not errors of law.
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Missouri recognizes coram nobis (State v. Larson, 79 S.W.3d 891 (Mo. banc 2002)) — read before citing.
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/coram%20nobis

### coram vobis

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: before you

Historically the twin of coram nobis used in a different court (Common Pleas rather than King’s Bench); later American usage applied it to a writ from a reviewing court to the trial court. Modern U.S. courts often treat the two names as interchangeable.

- **status**: corrected
- **note**: “Error correction in lower court” is only the later American usage; the writ also corrects errors of fact, not law.
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://openjurist.org/law-dictionary/coram-vobis

### corpus

- **sections**: Criminal; Property & torts; Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: body
- **meaning**: (1) Corpus delicti; (2) trust principal (vs. income); (3) the main body of a document.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/corpus

### corpus delicti

- **sections**: Evidence
- **literal**: body of the crime
- **meaning**: Proof that a crime was committed by someone (the injury and a criminal agency) — not a dead body. A confession alone usually cannot supply it.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/corpus%20delicti

### crimen falsi

- **sections**: Criminal
- **literal**: crime of falsehood
- **meaning**: Crimes involving dishonesty or false statement (perjury, forgery, fraud).
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Federal: FRE 609(a)(2) (automatic admissibility for impeachment).
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/crimen%20falsi

### cui bono

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: to whose benefit?
- **meaning**: Investigative principle that responsibility likely lies with whoever gains.

- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/cui%20bono

- **literal**: with the will annexed
- **meaning**: Administration where no executor serves.

- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/cum%20testamento%20annexo

- **sections**: Evidence
- **literal**: heaped up
- **meaning**: Additional evidence of the same kind on a point already proved; courts may exclude it as needlessly cumulative.

- **note**: English word, not a Latin term.

### de bene esse

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: of well-being (provisionally)
- **meaning**: Conditionally; provisionally, subject to later objection. A deposition de bene esse is taken to preserve testimony of a witness who may be unavailable at trial.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/de%20bene%20esse

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: in fact
- **meaning**: Existing in fact though not as a matter of law (e.g. de facto officer).

- **sources**: https://www.uscourts.gov/glossary

### de jure

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: by (in) law

- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.uscourts.gov/glossary

### de novo

- **sections**: Procedural; Appellate & writs
- **literal**: anew
- **meaning**: Fresh, from the start. Trial de novo is a completely new trial; de novo review gives no deference to the lower court’s ruling.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.uscourts.gov/glossary

### decretal

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Having the nature of a decree.
- **status**: source-checked

English term (some of Latin origin), not a Latin phrase. Dictionary gives the noun sense (a decree; historically a papal decree); the adjective sense “having the nature of a decree” is also used.

- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/decretal

### decretum

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: decree

- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/decretum

### delegata potestas non potest delegari

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: delegated power cannot be delegated

### demurrer

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings

- **mo**: Missouri: Rule 55.27. Federal: FRCP 12(b)(6).

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: after the fact

### ex post facto

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: from a thing done afterward

A law applied retroactively to a person’s detriment in criminal matters: punishing an act that was innocent when done, increasing the punishment after the fact, or removing a defense that existed when the act was done. The clause limits legislatures, not courts, and applies to criminal laws only.

- **status**: corrected
- **note**: Broader than “retroactively criminalizing conduct” — it also covers increased punishment and removed defenses.
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: U.S. Const. art. I, §§ 9-10; Mo. Const. art. I, § 13 (Missouri also bars laws “retrospective in operation”).
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/ex_post_facto

### ex rel. (ex relatione)

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: on the relation (information) of
- **meaning**: Case brought by the State on the information of a private party (State ex rel. X v. Y) — common in Missouri writ cases.
- **status**: reviewed
- **origin**: Latin

### exhibit

- **sections**: Evidence
- **literal**: held out; shown

- **note**: English word, not a Latin term.

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: from the stronger (reason)

Argument that if a conclusion holds in a weaker case, it holds even more strongly in a stronger one (if X is barred, then the greater X+ is barred all the more).

### ibid. (ibidem)

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: in the same place
- **meaning**: Same source as the immediately preceding citation.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/ibid

### id. (idem)

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: the same
- **meaning**: Same authority as the immediately preceding citation (legal citation form).
- **status**: reviewed
- **origin**: Latin

### idem sonans

- **sections**: Evidence
- **literal**: sounding the same
- **meaning**: A misspelled name that sounds the same as the correct one does not invalidate a document or charge.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/idem%20sonans

### ignorantia legis neminem excusat

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: ignorance of the law excuses no one

Not knowing the law is no defense. Variant: ignorantia juris non excusat. Narrow exceptions exist (e.g. reasonable reliance on an official statement of the law).

- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Missouri mistake-of-law exception for reasonable reliance on an official statement of the law: RSMo 562.031.2(2); defendant must inject the issue (text read on revisor.mo.gov 2026-10-06).
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/ignorantia%20legis%20neminem%20excusat

### imprimatur

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: let it be printed
- **meaning**: Official approval to publish.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/imprimatur

### in camera

- **sections**: Procedural; Evidence
- **literal**: in chambers
- **meaning**: Heard or reviewed privately by the judge, outside the presence of the jury and the public (e.g. in camera review of privileged documents).
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.uscourts.gov/glossary

### in claris non fit interpretatio

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: in clear things there is no interpretation
- **meaning**: Plain meaning: clear text is applied, not construed.
- **status**: reviewed
- **origin**: Latin

### in dubio pro reo

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: when in doubt, for the accused
- **meaning**: Doubt is resolved for the defendant — tied to the presumption of innocence; the rule of lenity is a related canon for ambiguous criminal statutes.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: It is broader than (and different from) the rule of lenity.
- **origin**: Latin

### in evidence

- **sections**: Evidence
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Admitted and before the trier of fact.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English phrase.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/in%20evidence

### in fine

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: at the end
- **meaning**: Finally; in short (literally “at the end”).
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: Meaning corrected 2026-10-06 against Merriam-Webster legal (“in short”); not limited to the end of a passage.
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/in%20fine

### in flagrante delicto

- **sections**: Criminal
- **literal**: while the crime is blazing
- **meaning**: Caught in the act.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/in%20flagrante%20delicto

### in forma pauperis

- **sections**: Procedural; Appellate & writs
- **literal**: in the manner of a pauper
- **meaning**: Court permission to file or appeal without paying the usual fees because the party cannot afford them. Applies to trial filings and appeals.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: Appellate entry said “appeal” only; it covers filing in the trial court too.
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Federal: 28 U.S.C. § 1915. Missouri: RSMo 514.040, suing as a poor person (text read on revisor.mo.gov 2026-10-06). See Definitions tab, “In forma pauperis (IFP)”.
- **sources**: https://www.uscourts.gov/glossary

### in gross

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: An easement benefiting a person rather than a parcel of land.
- **status**: reviewed
- **note**: Law French term, not Latin.
- **origin**: Law French

### in limine

- **sections**: Procedural; Evidence
- **literal**: at the threshold
- **meaning**: A pretrial motion for an advance ruling on evidence — usually to exclude it, sometimes to admit it.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: Not limited to excluding prejudicial evidence.
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: In Missouri a ruling in limine is interlocutory; you must still object (or offer the evidence) at trial to preserve the issue — see Definitions tab, “Preservation of error”. Pike County: motions in limine in writing with authority, filed 5 days before trial (45th L.R. 33.4).
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/in_limine

### in loco parentis

- **sections**: Family
- **literal**: in the place of a parent
- **meaning**: Assuming parental duties without formal adoption.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/in%20loco%20parentis

### in maxima potentia minima licentia

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: in the greatest power there is the least freedom
- **meaning**: Those with the most power must use it with the most restraint.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: Spelling (maxima) and meaning corrected: it does not mean “greater power includes lesser”.
- **origin**: Latin

### in pari delicto potior est conditio defendentis (possidentis)

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: in equal fault, the position of the defendant (possessor) is stronger
- **meaning**: Courts will not help one wrongdoer recover from an equally guilty one.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: Related to, but distinct from, the clean-hands doctrine.
- **origin**: Latin

### in pari materia

- **sections**: Procedural; Substantive
- **literal**: in a like matter
- **meaning**: Statutes on the same subject are read together as one body of law.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: Literal “on equal subject matter” → “in the same (or a like) matter”.
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/in%20pari%20materia

### in personam

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: against the person
- **meaning**: Jurisdiction over, or a judgment binding, a particular person or entity; enforceable elsewhere under full faith and credit.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/in_personam

### in propria persona / pro se

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: in one’s own proper person / for oneself
- **meaning**: Appearing for oneself without a lawyer. “Pro per” abbreviates in propria persona; Missouri and federal courts use “pro se”.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: Two different Latin phrases with the same practical meaning.
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.uscourts.gov/glossary; https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/pro_se; https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/in%20propria%20persona

### in re

- **sections**: Procedural; Criminal
- **literal**: in the matter of; concerning
- **meaning**: Used in case titles for proceedings about a thing, estate or status rather than adverse parties (estates, bankruptcy, guardianship, some habeas petitions).
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: Not specific to criminal matters (as the criminal-law list implied).
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/in%20re

### in rem

- **sections**: Procedural; Property & torts
- **literal**: against the thing
- **meaning**: An action against property itself, determining rights in it as against everyone, not just named parties.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/in_rem

### indicia

- **sections**: Evidence
- **literal**: signs; indications
- **meaning**: Circumstances that point to a fact (indicia of reliability, of ownership).
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/indicia

### infra

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: below
- **meaning**: Later in the document.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/infra

### infraction

- **sections**: Criminal
- **literal**: a breaking
- **meaning**: A minor violation, below a misdemeanor.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English word of Latin origin.
- **origin**: English
- **mo**: Missouri: RSMo 556.021 (infractions are not crimes) and 556.061(29) (text read on revisor.mo.gov 2026-10-06).
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/infraction

### inter alia

- **sections**: Procedural; Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: among other things
- **meaning**: Signals that the item mentioned is one of a larger group.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/inter_alia

### inter partes

- **sections**: Procedural
- **literal**: between the parties
- **meaning**: Involving adverse parties (contrast ex parte); a judgment inter partes binds only the parties, unlike one in rem.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: “Mutual agreement” is wrong; it describes proceedings or judgments between adverse parties.
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/inter%20partes

### inter se

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: among (between) themselves
- **meaning**: Rights and duties of the parties among themselves, as opposed to toward outsiders.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/inter%20se

### interdiction

- **sections**: Family
- **literal**: prohibition
- **meaning**: Civil-law declaration of incapacity; not a U.S. term for a restraining order.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: “Restraining order” is wrong in U.S. usage.
- **origin**: English

### interlocutory

- **sections**: Appellate & writs; Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Not final; made during the case (interlocutory order, interlocutory appeal).
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English term (some of Latin origin), not a Latin phrase.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/interlocutory

### invitee

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: A person on premises for the owner’s business benefit or as a member of the public on land open to the public.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English term, not Latin.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/invitee

### ipse dixit

- **sections**: Substantive; Evidence
- **literal**: he himself said it
- **meaning**: A bare assertion resting only on the speaker’s authority. Courts use it to reject expert opinion connected to the data only by the expert’s say-so.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/ipse%20dixit

### joint tenancy

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Co-ownership with right of survivorship.
- **status**: reviewed
- **note**: English term, not Latin.
- **origin**: English

### judgment

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: The court’s final determination of the parties’ rights.
- **status**: reviewed
- **note**: English term (some of Latin origin), not a Latin phrase.
- **origin**: English

### judicium

- **sections**: Evidence
- **literal**: judgment
- **meaning**: A court’s judgment or decision (historical term).
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/judicium

### jura eodem modo destituuntur quo constituuntur

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: rights are dissolved in the same way they are created
- **meaning**: A right created by a formal act must be ended by the same formality.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: Wrong word: “decipiuntur” means “are deceived”; the maxim uses destituuntur.
- **origin**: Latin

### jurisdiction (jurisdictio)

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: declaring of the law
- **meaning**: A court’s power to hear and decide a case.
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: “Speaking of law” → “declaring (saying) the law”.
- **origin**: English

### jus

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: law; right
- **meaning**: (1) Law as a whole; (2) a particular legal right. Used in many compounds (jus gentium, jus soli).
- **status**: corrected
- **note**: “Fundamental legal principle” is loose; it means law or a right.
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/jus

### jus accrescendi

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: right of accrual
- **meaning**: Right of survivorship among joint tenants.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/jus%20accrescendi

### jus cogens

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: compelling law
- **meaning**: Peremptory norms of international law from which no treaty may deviate (e.g. bans on genocide, slavery).
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.law.cornell.edu/wex/jus_cogens

### jus commune

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: common law

(1) The common law of England; (2) in legal history, the shared Roman-canon law of continental Europe; (3) general law as opposed to special law (jus singulare).

- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: Meaning confirmed, but the term is ambiguous — say which sense you mean.
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/jus%20commune

### jus gentium

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: law of nations
- **meaning**: International law; in Roman law, the rules common to all peoples, applied to non-citizens.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/jus%20gentium

### jus naturale

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: natural law
- **meaning**: Principles of justice thought to come from nature or reason rather than enactment.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/jus%20naturale

- **sections**: Family
- **literal**: right of blood
- **meaning**: Citizenship by parentage.

- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/jus%20sanguinis

- **origin**: Latin

### lex loci

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: law of the place
- **meaning**: Choice-of-law rule pointing to the law of a particular place.

- **sections**: Substantive

- **meaning**: The law of the place where a contract was made (or is to be performed).

- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Missouri does not apply the strict place-of-making rule; it uses the Restatement (Second) of Conflict of Laws “most significant relationship” test for contracts — confirm with a current case.

### lex loci delicti

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: law of the place of the wrong
- **meaning**: The law of the place where the tort occurred.

- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Missouri abandoned strict lex loci delicti for the most-significant-relationship test (Kennedy v. Dixon, 439 S.W.2d 173 (Mo. banc 1969)) — read before citing.

### lex neminem cogit ad impossibilia

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: the law compels no one to do the impossible
- **meaning**: Impossibility excuses performance.

- **sections**: Evidence; Appellate & writs

### nolo contendere

- **sections**: Evidence
- **literal**: I do not wish to contend

A plea that accepts conviction without admitting guilt. The plea generally cannot be used as an admission in a later civil case, though a civil suit can still be brought.

- **status**: corrected
- **note**: “Civil liability possible” needed clarifying: the plea is not an admission (FRE 410).
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Not available in Missouri state court: Rule 24.02 allows only not guilty, guilty, or not guilty by reason of mental disease or defect, and its Committee Note records the deletion of nolo contendere (read on the rules mirror 2026-10-06). Federal: FRE 410; Fed. R. Crim. P. 11(a)(3).
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/nolo%20contendere

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: name
- **meaning**: A name.

- **origin**: Latin

- **meaning**: Judgment entered contrary to the jury’s verdict because the evidence could not support it.

- **sections**: Substantive; Property & torts
- **literal**: the thing speaks for itself

Negligence may be inferred when the injury is of a kind that does not ordinarily happen without negligence, the instrumentality was under the defendant’s control, and the defendant has superior knowledge.

- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/trespass

### tutelage

- **sections**: Family
- **literal**: guardianship
- **meaning**: Guardianship of a minor or incompetent.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English word of Latin origin.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/tutelage

### uberrima fides

- **sections**: Maxims
- **literal**: utmost good faith
- **meaning**: Duty of full disclosure in certain contracts, notably insurance.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/uberrima%20fides

### ultima ratio

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: the last argument
- **meaning**: Last resort.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/ultima%20ratio

### ultra vires

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: beyond the powers
- **meaning**: Action beyond the authority granted to a corporation, agency or official.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/ultra%20vires

### unpublished opinion

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Opinion not printed in the reporter; usually non-precedential and subject to citation limits.
- **status**: reviewed
- **note**: English term (some of Latin origin), not a Latin phrase.
- **origin**: English

### usufruct

- **sections**: Property & torts
- **literal**: use and enjoyment
- **meaning**: Right to use another’s property and take its fruits without damaging it.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/usufruct

### vacate

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: To set aside a judgment or order.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English term (some of Latin origin), not a Latin phrase.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/vacate

### vel non

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: or not
- **meaning**: Whether or not (e.g. “negligence vel non”).
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/vel%20non

### venire de novo

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: that you cause to come anew
- **meaning**: Order for a new trial before a newly summoned jury.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/venire%20de%20novo

### verbatim (et literatim)

- **sections**: Evidence; Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: word for word (and letter for letter)
- **meaning**: Exact reproduction.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/verbatim

### vide

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: see
- **meaning**: See (citation signal).
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/vide

### viva voce

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: with the living voice
- **meaning**: Orally.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/viva%20voce

### viz. (videlicet)

- **sections**: Documents & pleadings
- **literal**: it is permitted to see; namely
- **meaning**: Namely.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/viz

### voir dire

- **sections**: Evidence; Procedural
- **literal**: to speak the truth
- **meaning**: Questioning of prospective jurors (or a witness) to test qualification and bias.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: Law French, not Latin.
- **origin**: Law French
- **sources**: https://www.uscourts.gov/glossary

### volenti non fit injuria

- **sections**: Substantive
- **literal**: to one who is willing, no wrong is done
- **meaning**: One who voluntarily assumes a known risk cannot recover for the resulting harm.
- **status**: source-checked
- **origin**: Latin
- **mo**: Missouri: implied secondary assumption of risk merges into comparative fault; express and implied primary assumption of risk survive (Coomer v. Kansas City Royals, 437 S.W.3d 184 (Mo. banc 2014)) — read before citing.
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/volenti%20non%20fit%20injuria

### writ

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: A written court order commanding an act or forbearance.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English term (some of Latin origin), not a Latin phrase.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/writ

### writ of error

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Historical writ for appellate review of errors of law on the record; replaced by appeal.
- **status**: source-checked
- **note**: English term (some of Latin origin), not a Latin phrase.
- **origin**: English
- **sources**: https://www.merriam-webster.com/legal/writ%20of%20error

### writ of execution

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Order to enforce a money judgment by levy on the debtor’s property.
- **status**: reviewed
- **note**: English term (some of Latin origin), not a Latin phrase.
- **origin**: English

### writ of supersedeas

- **sections**: Appellate & writs
- **literal**: —
- **meaning**: Order staying enforcement of a judgment pending appeal.
- **status**: reviewed
- **note**: English term (some of Latin origin), not a Latin phrase.
- **origin**: English


## deadlines

### Computing any period (civil)

- **deadline**: Exclude the trigger day; include the last day unless it’s a weekend/holiday; under 7 days, skip weekends/holidays
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 44.01(a)
- **note**: Same method in criminal cases under Rule 20.01(a).
- **area**: Civil

### Computing any period (criminal)

- **deadline**: Same as civil
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 20.01(a)
- **note**: Court may not enlarge time for a new-trial motion or appeal beyond the rules.
- **area**: Criminal

### Motion + notice of hearing served (criminal)

- **deadline**: At least 5 days before the hearing
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 20.01(d)
- **note**: Marion: e-filed papers must reach the clerk 2 business days before the hearing (10th L.R. 4.1).
- **area**: Criminal

### E-filed papers before a Marion hearing

- **deadline**: Received by clerk 2 business days before hearing
- **authority**: 10th Cir. L.R. 4.1
- **area**: Local

### Answer to petition

- **deadline**: 30 days after service (mail: 30 days after receipt filed; publication: 45 days)
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 55.25(a)
- **note**: A Rule 55.27 motion resets it to 10 days after the ruling.
- **area**: Civil

### Judgment final (civil, no after-trial motion)

- **deadline**: 30 days after entry
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.05(a)(1)
- **note**: With an after-trial motion: when last motion ruled (not before day 30) or day 90.
- **area**: Appeal

### Trial court control of judgment

- **deadline**: 30 days after entry
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 75.01
- **area**: Civil

### Notice of appeal

- **deadline**: 10 days after the judgment becomes final
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.04(a)
- **note**: $70 appellate docket fee.
- **area**: Appeal

### Late notice of appeal by special order

- **deadline**: Motion within 6 months after judgment final; then notice within 10 days of order
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.07(a)
- **note**: Must show delay not due to culpable negligence.
- **area**: Appeal

### Order transcript

- **deadline**: 10 days after notice of appeal (written order)
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.12(c)(1)
- **note**: Pike: reporter starts only after cash deposit (45th L.R. 10). Certificate of order/payment within 10 days of payment.
- **area**: Appeal

### File record on appeal

- **deadline**: 30 days (legal file only) / 90 days (with transcript) after notice of appeal
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 81.19
- **note**: Extensions: Rule 81.20 + E.D. Rule 340 reporter statement. Paper only in E.D. (Rule 330).
- **area**: Appeal

### Notify E.D. clerk of events affecting appeal

- **deadline**: Within 5 days of the event (incl. record problems)
- **authority**: E.D. Special Rule 310
- **area**: Appeal

### Appellant’s brief

- **deadline**: 60 days after record filed
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 84.05(a)
- **note**: Respondent 30 days after; reply 15 days after. E.D. limit 15,500 words (Rule 360).
- **area**: Appeal

### Cure a dismissal-docket default

- **deadline**: By the date in the clerk’s notice (at least 15 days)
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 84.08(a)
- **area**: Appeal

### Motion for new trial (criminal jury)

- **deadline**: 15 days after verdict (+ up to 10 days on good cause)
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 29.11
- **note**: Must list every error to preserve it; unruled after 90 days = denied.
- **area**: Criminal

### Instruction objections

- **deadline**: Before the jury retires
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 28.03
- **note**: Repeat in the motion for new trial.
- **area**: Criminal

### Proposed jury instructions (Marion)

- **deadline**: At the pretrial conference
- **authority**: 10th Cir. L.R. 53.1
- **area**: Local

### Motions in limine (Pike)

- **deadline**: Filed + noticed 5 days before trial, with authority
- **authority**: 45th Cir. L.R. 33.4
- **area**: Local

### Motion to suppress

- **deadline**: Before trial
- **authority**: Mo. Sup. Ct. R. 24.05
- **note**: Marion: all motions disposed before trial (10th L.R. 67.7).
- **area**: Criminal

### Trial after bail denied as danger

- **deadline**: Within 120 days of arraignment — written request AT arraignment
- **authority**: RSMo 544.676.3
- **note**: Only if bail was denied on the danger ground.
- **area**: Criminal

### MCHR complaint

- **deadline**: 180 days after the discriminatory act
- **authority**: RSMo 213.075.1
- **note**: EEOC filing is deemed filed with MCHR (213.075.2).
- **area**: Employment

### EEOC charge (deferral state)

- **deadline**: 300 days after the act
- **authority**: 42 U.S.C. 2000e-5(e)(1)
- **note**: 180 days where no state agency proceedings.
- **area**: Employment

### MHRA suit after right-to-sue

- **deadline**: 90 days from the letter, no later than 2 years after the act
- **authority**: RSMo 213.111.1
- **area**: Employment

### FMLA eligibility / designation notices (employer)

- **deadline**: 5 business days
- **authority**: 29 C.F.R. 825.300(b), (d)
- **area**: Employment

### Federal service of process

- **deadline**: 90 days after filing the complaint
- **authority**: Fed. R. Civ. P. 4(m)
- **area**: Federal

### Federal answer

- **deadline**: 21 days after service (60 if service waived)
- **authority**: Fed. R. Civ. P. 12(a)
- **area**: Federal

### Amend once as of course (federal)

- **deadline**: 21 days after serving, or 21 days after answer / Rule 12(b),(e),(f) motion
- **authority**: Fed. R. Civ. P. 15(a)(1)
- **area**: Federal

### Supplemental-claim tolling after dismissal

- **deadline**: Refile in state court within 30 days after dismissal (or longer state period)
- **authority**: 28 U.S.C. 1367(d)
- **area**: Federal

### § 1983 limitations (Missouri)

- **deadline**: 5 years
- **authority**: RSMo 516.120(4)
- **note**: Per the book’s 516.120 entry and Sulik (8th Cir. 2005).
- **area**: Federal

### Bodycam footage from a nonpublic place

- **deadline**: Notice to each depicted non-officer; wait at least 10 days before display or disclosure
- **authority**: RSMo 610.100.8
- **note**: Violation exposes you to civil damages.
- **area**: Records
